represents the number of connected components of
M
,
B_{n}=0
, and if
M
is contractible then
B_{r}=0
for
r=1,\ldots,n-1
.

In particular, let
M
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then the dimension of the spaces of harmonic vector fields (i.e. vector fields
\vec{u}
with
\mathrm{div}\,\vec{u}=0
,
\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{u}=0
) with vanishing tangential (normal) part on
\partial M
is
B_{2}
(the number of holes in
M
) (resp.
B_{1}
— the number of independent “circuits” in
M
).

Let
\mathcal{H}(M)
denote the set of all
h\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
with
dh=0
and
\delta h=0
.

Chapter 3
Potential Theory for Variable Exponent Spaces

We shall use a number of results on classical integral operators in variable exponent spaces, see
[
DHHR11
]
.

3.1.
Maximal function and Riesz potential estimates

Let

I_{\alpha}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{f(y)}{|x-y|^{n-\alpha}}\,dy\qquad\text{ and }\qquad Mf\left(x\right)=\sup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in B\\
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ is a ball}\end{subarray}}\fint_{B}\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert\,dy.

For an exponent
p\left(\cdot\right),
we write
p\in\mathcal{P}^{log}(\Omega)
, respectively
p\in\mathcal{P}^{log}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
if
p
satisfies (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) with
M
replaced by
\Omega
, respectively
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with
\mathrm{dist}(x,y)
the standard Euclidian distance. Also,
c_{\mathrm{log}}(p)
denotes the best constant for which (
1.2
) holds.

Lemma 3.1
.

(Corollary 4.3.11.,
[
DHHR11
]
) Let
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{log}}(\Omega)
with
p^{-}>1
. Then there exists
K>0
only depending on
c_{\mathrm{log}}(p)
and the dimension
n
such that

\|Mf\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(\Omega)}\leq K(p^{-})^{\prime}\|f\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(\Omega)}

for all
f\in L^{p(\cdot)}(\Omega)
.

Let
B_{R}
be a ball with radius
R
and
B_{R}(x)
be the ball of radius
R
centered at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We shall use the following standard estimate.

Lemma 3.2
.

(Lemma 6.1.4.,
[
DHHR11
]
) For
R,\alpha>0
there holds

\int\limits_{B_{R}(x)}\frac{|f(y)|}{|x-y|^{n-\alpha}}\,dy\leq C(\alpha)R^{\alpha}Mf(x).

In
[
DHHR11
]
this result is stated for
0<\alpha<n
, and for
\alpha\geq n
this estimate is trivial.

For
n=2
we shall also use the following estimate for the logarithmic potential which is proved exactly as the previous result:

(3.1)

\int\limits_{B_{R}(x)}|f(y)|\log\frac{R}{|x-y|}\,dy\leq C(n)R^{n}Mf(x).

Corollary 3.3
.

For a function with support in
B_{R}
,
R\leq 1
, and
\alpha>0
there holds

\|I_{\alpha}f\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(B_{R})}\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p),\alpha)R^{\alpha}\|f\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(B_{R})}.

Proof.

Immediately follows from Lemmas
3.1
,
3.2
.
∎

For the logarithmic potential

I_{\mathrm{log},R}f(x)=\int\limits_{B_{R}}f(y)\log\frac{|x-y|}{R}\,dy

an analogue of Corollary
3.3
is given by the estimate

(3.2)

\|I_{\mathrm{log},R}f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}\leq C(n)R^{n}\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}},

which follows from (
3.1
) and the maximal function estimate.

Lemma 3.4
.

(Theorem 6.1.9 (page 203),
[
DHHR11
]
) Let
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{log}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
0<\alpha<n
,
1<p_{-}\leq p_{+}<n/\alpha
, and
1/p^{\sharp}=1/p-\alpha/n
. Then

\|I_{\alpha}f\|_{p^{\sharp}(\cdot)}\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p),\alpha)\|f\|_{p(\cdot)}.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{log}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Denote
\varkappa=n/(n-s)
,
s\in[1,n-1]
. Let
\operatorname{supp}\,f\subset\overline{B}_{R}
,
R\leq 1
. Then

\|I_{s}f\|_{\varkappa p(\cdot),B_{R}}\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|f\|_{p(\cdot)}R^{s/p_{-}^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Let
q
be such that
\varkappa p=nq/(n-sq)
, or

\frac{1}{p}-\frac{s}{np}=\frac{1}{q}-\frac{s}{n}.

This gives

\frac{1}{q}=\frac{s}{n}+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{s}{np},\quad q=\frac{np}{n+sp-s},

where
1<q<\min(p,n/s)
and

\displaystyle\frac{n}{s}-q=\frac{n}{s}\cdot\frac{n-s}{n+sp-s}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{np_{+}},

\displaystyle q-1=\frac{(p-1)(n-s)}{n+s(p-1)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(p_{-}-1)}{np_{-}}.

Then by Lemma
3.4
and the Hölder inequality,

\displaystyle\|I_{s}f\|_{\varkappa p(\cdot),B_{R}}\leq C\|f\|_{q(\cdot),B_{R}}

\displaystyle\leq 2C\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}\|1\|_{np^{\prime}(\cdot)/s,B_{R}}

\displaystyle=2C\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}\|1\|_{p^{\prime}(\cdot),B_{R}}^{s/n}.

It remains to use the standard estimate
\|1\|_{z(\cdot),B_{R}}\lesssim R^{n/\bar{z}}
for
z\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{log}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for the last factor, where
\bar{z}
is the average of
z
over
B_{R}
.
∎

3.2.
Interpolation in variable exponent spaces

In this Section
B_{R}
is ball of radius
R
, if it centered at
x^{n}=0
we denote
B_{R}^{+}=B_{R}\cap\{x^{n}>0\}
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
f\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(B_{R})
satisfy
f=0
on
\partial B_{R}
. Then

\displaystyle\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))R\|\nabla f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}},

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\varkappa p(\cdot),B_{R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))R^{1/(np_{-}^{\prime})}\|\nabla f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}.

The same estimate is valid in if
B_{R}
is replaced by
B_{R}^{+}
and
\partial B_{R}
is replaced by
\partial B_{R}\cap\{x_{n}\geq 0\}
.

Proof.

We prove this for
B_{R}^{+}
. Consider the even extension of
f
to
\{x_{n}<0\}
across
\{x_{n}=0\}
, and then extend
f
by zero outside
B_{R}
. We denote this extension again by
f
. Then

|f(x)|\leq C(n)I_{1}[|\nabla f|](x).

It remains to use Corollary
3.3
and Lemma
3.5
.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
f\in W^{k,p(\cdot)}(B_{R})
vanish on
\partial B_{R}
together with all its partial derivatives up to the order
k-1
. Then

\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}R^{k-l}\|\nabla^{l}f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p),k)\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}.

The same estimate is valid if
B_{R}
is replaced by
B_{R}^{+}
and
\partial B_{R}
is replaced by
\partial B_{R}\cap\{x_{n}>0\}
.

Proof.

Follows from applying Corollary
3.6
to
\nabla^{l}f
for
l=k-1
, …,
0
.
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

For
f\in W^{k,p(\cdot)}(B_{R})
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
there holds

\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}R^{l+1-k}\|\nabla^{l}f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}\leq CR\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}+CR^{-n-1}\|f\|_{1,B_{R}}

with a constant
C=C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p),k)
.

Proof.

The first statement immediately follows from Lemmas
3.1
,
3.2
. We use the Sobolev representation (see
[
Maz11
]
Section 1.1.10): there exists a polynomial
Q(x)
such that

f(x)-Q(x)=\sum_{\alpha=k}\int\limits_{B_{R}}\frac{\varphi_{\alpha}(x,y)\nabla^{\alpha}f(y)}{|x-y|^{n-k}},

where for
l=0,\ldots k-1
and
x,y\in B_{R}
there holds

R^{l}|\nabla^{l}Q(x)|\leq CR^{-n}\int\limits_{B_{R}}|f(y)|\,dy,\qquad R^{l}|\nabla_{x}^{l}\varphi_{\alpha}(x,y)|\leq C.

Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}R^{l}\|\nabla^{l}Q\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}

\displaystyle\leq CR^{-n}\int\limits_{{B}_{R}}|f(y)|\,dy,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}R^{l}\|\nabla^{l}(f-Q)\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\sum_{\alpha+\beta=l}R^{-\alpha}\|I_{k-\beta}[\nabla^{k}f]\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R})}

\displaystyle\leq CR^{k}\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{R}},

whence the second claim.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. On a Riemannian
C^{k,1}
manifold for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a constant
C(\varepsilon)
such that any
\omega\in W^{k,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfies

\|\omega\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}\leq\varepsilon\|\omega\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}+C(\varepsilon)\|\omega\|_{1,M}.

Proof.

To prove the interpolation estimates on manifolds it is sufficient to consider one coordinate chart. Covering the support of
\omega\eta_{\alpha}
, where
\eta_{\alpha}
is an element of the partition of unity, in this chart by balls with sufficiently small radius
C\varepsilon
and finite overlap, using in each of these cubes the interpolation estimate, and summing over these cubes, we get the required estimate.
∎

3.3.
Calderon-Zygmund operators

The following theory of Calderon-Zygmund operators in variable exponent spaces was developed by L. Diening and M. Růžička, see [
[
DHHR11
]
, Section 6.3].

Definition.
A kernel
k(\cdot,\cdot)
is called a standard kernel if it satisfies the following estimates for all distinct
x,y
and for all
z
with
|x-z|<|x-y|/2
:

\displaystyle|k(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq c|x-y|^{-n},

\displaystyle|k(x,y)-k(z,y)|

\displaystyle\leq c|x-z|^{\delta}\cdot|x-y|^{-n-\delta},

\displaystyle|k(y,x)-k(y,z)|

\displaystyle\leq c|x-z|^{\delta}\cdot|x-y|^{-n-\delta},

with some
\delta>0
. For
\varepsilon>0
define the integral operator
T_{\varepsilon}
on
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by

T_{\varepsilon}f(x)=\int\limits_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}k(x,y)f(y)\,dy.

Lemma 3.10
.

(Corollary 6.3.13.
[
DHHR11
]
) Let
k
be a standard kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Assume that
N(x,z):=k(x,x-z)
is homogeneous of degree
-n
in
z
and that for every
x
the integral of
N(x,z)
over the sphere
|z|=1
equals zero. Let
p\in\mathcal{P}^{log}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
1<p_{-}\leq p_{+}<\infty
. Then the operators
T_{\varepsilon}
are uniformly bounded on
L^{p(\cdot)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with respect to
\varepsilon>0
. Moreover,
Tf(x):=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}T_{\varepsilon}f(x)
exists almost everywhere and
T_{\varepsilon}f\to Tf
in
L^{p(\cdot)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. In particular,
T
is bounded on
L^{p(\cdot)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

3.4.
Potentials in the full space

For
n\geq 3
denote

K_{0}(z)=\frac{|z|^{2-n}}{(2-n)\omega_{n}},

where
\omega_{n}
is the
(n-1)
-volume of the unit sphere
|x|=1
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For
n=2
we set

K_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|.

Further we shall mainly deal with the case
n\geq 3
and write the corresponding representations. So by default
n\geq 3
. All the results are also valid for the two-dimensional case, we mark the cases when the corresponding 2D result deviates from the result for
n\geq 3
.

Let
P[f]
be the Newtonian potential of
f
,

P[f](x)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{0}(x-y)f(y)\,dy=-(n-2)^{-1}\omega_{n}^{-1}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|x-y|^{2-n}f(y)\,dy.

for
n\geq 3
and the logarithmic potential

P[f](x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(y)\log|x-y|\,dy\quad\text{for}\quad n=2.

Let
Q_{\alpha}[f]
be the potential solving
\triangle Q_{\alpha}[f]=-f_{,\alpha}
,

Q_{\alpha}[f]=-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{0,\alpha}(x-y)f(y)\,dy=\omega_{n}^{-1}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|x-y|^{-n}(y_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha})f(y)\,dy.

Let
Q[e]
,
e=e^{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1,\ldots,n
, be the potential solving
\triangle u=-e^{\alpha}_{,\alpha}
, that is

\displaystyle Q[e](x)=Q_{\alpha}[e^{\alpha}](x)

\displaystyle=-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{0,\alpha}(x-y)e^{\alpha}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\omega_{n}^{-1}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|x-y|^{-n}(y_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha})e^{\alpha}(y)\,dy.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Equip
W^{s,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R})
with the norm

\|u\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),B_{2R}}=\sum_{k=0}^{s}R^{k-s}\|\nabla^{k}u\|^{*}_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}},

The following two lemmas summarize the basic properties of the volume potentials in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in variable exponent spaces. Let
\varkappa=n/(n-1)
. For
n=2
we define

(3.3)

c(f;B_{2R})=\frac{\log(2R)}{2\pi}\int\limits_{B_{2R}}f\,dx.

Lemma 3.11
.

For
f\in L^{p}(B_{2R})
with support in
B_{2R}
there holds

\displaystyle\|Q_{\alpha}[f]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|P[f]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))R\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|P[f]\|^{*}_{1,\varkappa p(\cdot),B_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|P[f]\|^{*}_{2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}}.

For
n=2
the estimates for
P[f]
are valid with
P[f]
replaced by
P[f]-c(f;B_{2R})
.

Proof.

There holds

\displaystyle|P[f]|\leq C(n)I_{2}[f],\qquad|\nabla P[f]|,|Q_{\alpha}[f]|\leq C(n)I_{1}[f],

\displaystyle|\nabla Q_{\alpha}[f]|,\ |\nabla^{2}P[f]|\leq C(n)(|f|+|\mathrm{CZ}[f]|),

where
\mathrm{CZ}[g]
is a Calderon-Zygmund type singular integral operator. It remains to use the Calderon-Zygmund theory in variable exponent spaces from Lemma
3.10
, together with Corollary
3.3
and Lemma
3.5
. For the third estimate we also use the obvious relation
P[f]\leq C(n)RI_{1}[f]
to get the estimate of
P[f]
in
L^{\varkappa p(\cdot)}(B_{2R})
.

For
n=2
we have

P[f]-c(f;B_{2R})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{B_{2R}}f(y)\log\frac{|x-y|}{2R}\,dy.

It remains to use the estimate (
3.2
) to evaluate the norm of
P[f]
in
L^{p(\cdot)}(B_{2R})
, with the rest of the estimates being the same as for
n\geq 3
.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

It is easy to see that for the additional term arising in the planar case we have

|c(f;B_{2R})|\leq C(p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))R^{2}\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}}|\log(2R)|.

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R})
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, with support in
B_{2R}
. Then

\|P[f]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}}\leq C\|f\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),B_{2R}}.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R})
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, with support in
B_{2R}
. Then

\|Q_{\alpha}[f]\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}}\leq C\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),B_{2R}}^{*}.

For
n=2
the estimate for
P[f]
is valid with
P[f]
replaced by
P[f]-c(f;B_{2R})
.

Proof.

Follows from the standard relations

(P[f])_{,\alpha}=P[f_{,\alpha}],\quad(Q_{\alpha}[f])_{,\beta}=Q_{\alpha}[f_{,\beta}],

and estimates of Lemma
3.11
.
∎

For
f\in L^{p}(B_{2R})
with support in
B_{2R}
, the potential
v=P[f]\in W^{2,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\nabla v\cdot\nabla\zeta+f\zeta)\,dx=0

for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and the potential
w=Q_{\alpha}[f]\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\nabla v\cdot\nabla\zeta+f\zeta_{,\alpha})\,dx=0

for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The potentials
P[f]
and
Q_{\alpha}[f]
decay at infinity with the exception of the case
n=2
where
P[f]
decays at infinity under the additional condition of zero mean of
f
over
B_{2R}
.

3.5.
Half-space potentials

Let
\rho
be a positive number. In all the subsequent paper but one place (namely, in the proof of Corollary
3.8
) we use the value
\rho=1
. Denote

\triangle_{\rho}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial(x^{j})^{2}}+\rho^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial(x^{n})^{2}},\quad K_{0}(x;\rho)=\rho^{-1}K_{0}(x^{\prime},\rho^{-1}x^{n}).

There holds
\triangle_{\rho}K_{0}(x;\rho)=\delta(x)
. Clearly,
K_{0}(x;1)=K_{0}(x)
. In this Section
C
is a constant which depends on
n
,
p_{-}
,
p_{+}
,
c_{\mathrm{log}}(p)
, and on
\rho
.

Let
B_{2R}^{+}=B_{2R}\cap\{x_{n}>0\}
,
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}=B_{2R}\cap\{x_{n}\geq 0\}
.

We can assume that the the variable exponent
p(\cdot)
is extended to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by first taking even reflection across
\{x_{n}=0\}
if we work in boundary patches, and then using the standard McShane-Whitney extension procedure from the ball
B_{2R}
, which keeps the same bounds and log-Hölder modulus of continuity:

p(x)=\max(\sup_{y\in B_{2R}}(p(y)-\omega(|x-y|)),p_{-}),

where
\omega(t)=L(\log(e+t^{-1}))^{-1}
, is the log-Hölder modulus of continuity of
p
on
B_{2R}
. In particular, for boundary patches this extension is even with respect to
x^{n}
.

Let

•

y^{*}
denote the reflection of
y
across
y_{n}=0
,
(y^{\prime},y^{n})^{*}=(y^{\prime},-y^{n})
;

•

\mathcal{E}^{D}
denote the odd extension of a function across
y_{n}=0
from the upper half-space:

\mathcal{E}^{D}[f](y^{\prime},y_{n})=-f(y^{\prime},-y_{n}),\quad y_{n}<0;

•

\mathcal{E}^{N}
denote the even extension of a function across
y_{n}=0
from the upper half-space,

\mathcal{E}^{N}[f](y^{\prime},y_{n})=f(y^{\prime},-y_{n}),\quad y_{n}<0;

•

g_{D}(x,y):=K_{0}(x-y;\rho)-K_{0}(x-y^{*};\rho)
;

•

g_{N}(x,y):=K_{0}(x-y;\rho)+K_{0}(x-y^{*};\rho)
;

For a scalar function
f
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
we set

\displaystyle P^{D}[f]=P[\mathcal{E}^{D}g]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}g_{D}(x,y)f(y)\,dy,

\displaystyle P^{N}[f]=P[\mathcal{E}^{N}g]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}g_{N}(x,y)f(y)\,dy,

\displaystyle Q^{D}_{\alpha}[f]=Q_{\alpha}[\mathcal{E}^{D}f]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\frac{\partial g_{D}(x,y)}{\partial y^{\alpha}}f(y)\,dy,\quad\alpha<n,

\displaystyle Q^{D}_{\alpha}[f]=Q_{\alpha}[\mathcal{E}^{N}f]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\frac{\partial g_{D}(x,y)}{\partial y^{\alpha}}f(y)\,dy,\quad\alpha=n,

\displaystyle Q^{N}_{\alpha}[f]=Q_{\alpha}[\mathcal{E}^{N}f]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\frac{\partial g_{N}(x,y)}{\partial y^{\alpha}}f(y)\,dy,\quad\alpha<n,

\displaystyle Q^{D}_{\alpha}[f]=Q_{\alpha}[\mathcal{E}^{D}f]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\frac{\partial g_{N}(x,y)}{\partial y^{\alpha}}f(y)\,dy,\quad\alpha=n.

For
e=\{e^{\alpha}\}
we set

Q^{D}[e]=Q^{D}_{\alpha}[e^{\alpha}],\quad Q^{N}[e]=Q^{N}_{\alpha}[e^{\alpha}].

Note that

•

the potential
P^{D}[f]
solves the Dirichlet problem
\triangle_{\rho}u=f
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
,
u=0
on
x_{n}=0
;

•

the potential
P^{N}[f]
solves the Neumann problem
\triangle_{\rho}u=f
,
\partial u/\partial x_{n}=0
on
x_{n}=0
;

•

the potential
Q^{D}[e]
solves the Dirichlet problem
\triangle_{\rho}u=-(e^{\alpha})_{,\alpha}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
,
u=0
on
x_{n}=0
,

•

the potential
Q^{N}[e]
solves the Neumann problem
\triangle_{\rho}u=-(e^{\alpha})_{,\alpha}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
,
\rho^{2}\partial u/\partial x^{n}+e^{n}=0
on
x^{n}=0
.

Denote
\nabla_{\rho}v=(\partial v/\partial x^{1},\ldots,\partial v/\partial x^{n-1},\rho\,\partial v/\partial x^{n})
.
For
f\in L^{p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})
with support in
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
the potential
v=P^{D}[f]
belongs to
W^{2,p(\cdot)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
, has zero trace on
\{x^{n}=0\}
and satisfies

(3.4)

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}(\nabla_{\rho}v\cdot\nabla_{\rho}\zeta+f\zeta)\,dx=0,

for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
, while
v=P^{N}[f]
belongs to
W^{2,p(\cdot)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
and satisfies (
3.4
) for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+})
. The potential
w=Q_{\alpha}^{D}[f]
belongs to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
, has zero trace on
\{x^{n}=0\}
and satisfies

(3.5)

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}(\nabla_{\rho}w\cdot\nabla_{\rho}\zeta+\zeta f_{,\alpha})\,dx=0

for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
, while
w=Q^{N}_{\alpha}[f]
belongs to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
and satisfies (
3.5
) for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+})
. All these potentials decay at infinity with the exception of
P^{N}[f]
in the dimension
n=2
, which requires additionally that the mean of
f
over
B_{2R}^{+}
is zero.

In the following statements
P^{*}
will be either
P^{D}
or
P^{N}
and
Q^{*}
will be either
Q^{D}
or
Q^{N}
. In
W^{s,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})
we shall use the norm

\|u\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}=\sum_{k=0}^{s}R^{k-s}\|\nabla^{k}u\|^{*}_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

For
n=2
we denote

(3.6)

c(f;B_{2R}^{+})=c(\mathcal{E}^{N}f;B_{2R})=\frac{\log(2R)}{2\pi}\int\limits_{B_{2R}^{+}}f\,dx.

Lemma 3.14
.

For
f\in L^{p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})
with support in
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
there holds

\displaystyle\|Q^{*}_{\alpha}[f]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}},

\displaystyle\|P^{*}[f]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}

\displaystyle\leq CR\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}},

\displaystyle\|P^{*}[f]\|^{*}_{1,\varkappa p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

The potentials
P^{D}[f]
and
Q^{D}_{\alpha}[f]
have zero trace on
x^{n}=0
. For
n=2
the estimates for
P^{N}[f]
are valid with
P^{N}[f]
replaced by
P^{N}[f]-c(f;B_{2R}^{+})
, while the rest of the estimates is unchanged.

Proof.

These estimates follow from Lemma
3.11
and the definition of the half-space potentials
P^{*}
and
Q^{*}
via even or odd extension. Extending
P^{D}[f]
and
Q^{D}_{\alpha}[f]
by zero to
x^{n}<0
we get functions from
W^{1,1}(B_{4R})
by the corresponding constant exponent result. But this and the log-Hölder property of the exponent
p(\cdot)
imply the zero trace property.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
f\in L^{p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})
with support in
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
. Then

\|P^{*}[f]\|^{*}_{2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

Let
f\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})
with support in
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
. Then

\|Q^{*}_{\alpha}[f]\|^{*}_{2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f\|_{1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}^{*}.

For
n=2
the estimate for
P^{N}[f]
is valid with
P^{N}[f]
replaced by
P^{N}[f]-c(f;B_{2R}^{+})
, while the rest of the estimates is unchanged.

Proof.

The first estimate follows from Calderon-Zygmund estimates in variable exponent spaces and Corollary
3.3
.

We prove the second estimate for
Q^{N}
, the case of
Q^{D}
is similar. Consider the integral

(3.7)

h_{\alpha}(x)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\frac{\partial g_{N}(x,y)}{\partial y^{\alpha}}f(y)\,dy

where
f\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
and
f
is supported in
G_{2R}
.

For
\alpha=1,\ldots,n-1
we can integrate by parts in tangential direction to get

h_{\alpha}(x)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}g_{N}(x,y)f_{,\alpha}\,dy=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{0}(x-y;\rho)\mathcal{E}^{N}f_{,\alpha}(y)\,dy.

From Calderon-Zygmund estimates in variable exponent spaces it follows that

\|h_{\alpha}\|^{*}_{2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|\nabla f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

Now consider the integral (
3.7
) for
\alpha=n
, which we can write as

h_{n}(x)=-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{0,n}(z;\rho)\mathcal{E}^{D}f(x-z)\,dz.

For
\alpha=1,\ldots,n-1
(the tangential derivatives) we have

h_{n,\alpha}(x)=-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{0,n}(x-y;\rho)\mathcal{E}^{D}f_{,\alpha}(y)\,dy,

thus by the Calderon-Zygmund theorem in variable exponent spaces

\|\nabla h_{n,\alpha}\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f_{,\alpha}\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

This guarantees that

\|h_{n,\alpha\beta}\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|\nabla f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha+\beta<2n.

Now, the potential
h_{n}
satisfies the integral identity (see, for instance
[
Mor66
]
)

\int\limits_{B_{2R}^{+}}\bigl[(\rho^{2}h_{n,n}+f)\zeta_{,n}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}h_{n,\alpha}\zeta_{,\alpha}\bigr]\,dx=0

for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{B}_{2R}^{+})
. Integrating by parts, we get

\int\limits_{B_{2R}^{+}}(\rho^{2}h_{n,n}+f)\zeta_{,n}\,dx=\int\limits_{B_{2R}^{+}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}h_{n,\alpha\alpha}\zeta\,dx=0.

This means that the weak derivative
h_{n,nn}
exists and satisfies the relation

\rho^{2}h_{n,nn}=-f-\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n-1}h_{n,\alpha\alpha}.

Thus,

\|h_{n,nn}\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|\nabla f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

We have proved that

\|\nabla^{2}h_{\alpha}\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|\nabla f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}},\quad\alpha=1,\ldots,n.

Combining with the previous estimate

\|Q^{*}_{\alpha}[f]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}},

we get the result.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, with support in
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
. Then

\|P^{*}[f]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

Let
f\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})

s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, with support in
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
. Then

\|Q_{\alpha}^{*}[f]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}^{*}.

For
n=2
the estimate for
P^{N}[f]
is valid with
P^{N}[f]
replaced by
P^{N}[f]-c(f;B_{2R}^{+})
, while the rest of the estimates is unchanged.

Proof.

We show the estimate only for the potential
Q^{*}
, the estimate for
P^{*}
is similar. We argue by induction. The base of induction for
s=0
is already known. Let the estimate be known for all values
s=1,\ldots,s_{0}
and we show it for
s=s_{0}+1
. Since we already know that

\|Q_{\alpha}^{*}[f]\|^{*}_{s_{0}+1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f\|_{s_{0},p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}^{*},

we only have to establish the estimate

\|\nabla^{s_{0}+2}Q^{*}[f]\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f\|_{s_{0}+1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}^{*}.

For a multiindex
\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n-1},\beta_{n})
let
D^{\beta}u=D^{\beta_{1}}_{x_{1}}\ldots D^{\beta_{n}}_{x_{n}}
. Note that for all multiindices
\beta
with
|\beta|\leq s_{0}
and
\beta_{n}=0
there holds

\displaystyle D^{\beta}Q_{\alpha}^{*}[f]

\displaystyle=Q^{*}_{\alpha}[D^{\beta}f]\in W^{2,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+}),

\displaystyle\|D^{\beta}Q_{\alpha}^{*}[f]\|^{*}_{2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|D^{\beta}f\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

Therefore, for
|\beta|=s_{0}+1
with
\beta_{n}\leq 1
we get

(3.8)

\|D^{\beta}Q_{\alpha}^{*}[f]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C\|f\|_{s_{0}+1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}^{*}.

Let us show, again by induction in
\beta_{n}
, that (
3.8
) holds for all
\beta
with
|\beta|=s_{0}+1
. Assume that (
3.8
) holds for all
\beta_{n}\leq j_{0}
,
j_{0}\leq s_{0}
. We show that it holds for
\beta_{n}\leq j_{0}+1
. Applying
D^{\beta}
with
|\beta|=s_{0}-1
and
\beta_{n}=j_{0}-1
to

\rho^{2}(Q_{\alpha}^{*}[f])_{,nn}=-\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}(Q_{\alpha}^{*}[f])_{,ll}-f_{,\alpha},

we get

\rho^{2}D^{\beta}(Q_{\alpha}[f])_{,nn}=-\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}D^{\beta}(Q[f])_{,ll}-D^{\beta}f_{,\alpha}.

The right-hand side contains the
(s_{0}+1)
-th derivatives of
Q_{\alpha}[f]
with
\beta_{n}=j_{0}-1
, thus by the assumption of induction the left-hand side belongs to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})
, and (
3.8
) is satisfied for
|\beta|=s_{0}+1
with
\beta_{n}=j_{0}+1
.
∎

Let

\displaystyle U_{\rho}[f](x)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}g_{N}(x,y)f(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(2-n)\omega_{n}\rho}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}(|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}+(x^{n}/\rho)^{2})^{\frac{2-n}{2}}f(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime},

where the last expression is given for
n\geq 3
. This potential represents a solution of the Neumann problem
\triangle U[f]=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
,
\rho^{2}\partial U[f]/\partial x^{n}=f
on
x_{n}=0
. Clearly,
U_{\rho}[f](x)=\rho^{-1}U_{1}[f](x^{\prime},x_{n}/\rho)
, where
U_{1}
is the standard single-layer potential corresponding to the Laplace operator.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
f\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(B_{2R}^{+})
,
s=0,1,2,\ldots
have support in
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
. Then

\|U_{\rho}[f(\cdot,0)]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p),s)\|f\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

If
n=2
then the right-hand side is multiplied by the factor
(1+\log R^{-1})
.

Proof.

There holds

\displaystyle U_{\rho}[f(\cdot,0)]

\displaystyle=-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\left(\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial(g_{N}(x,y)f(y))}{\partial y^{n}}dy^{n}\right)\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-Q_{n}^{N}[f]-P^{N}\left[\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{n}}\right].

The required properties of
U_{\rho}[f]
follow then from Lemma
3.16
. For
n=2
we also use the estimate

\displaystyle\|c(f_{,n};B_{2R}^{+})\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}=R^{-s-2}\|c(f_{,n};B_{2R}^{+})\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}

\displaystyle\leq C|\log(2R)|R^{s}\|f_{,n}\|_{p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}\leq C|\log(2R)|\|f\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),B_{2R}^{+}}.

which follows by the definition of corresponding norms and the log-Hölder property of the exponent
p(\cdot)
.
∎

Using this simple statement we prove the following

Corollary 3.18
.

Let
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
,
M
be of the class
C^{s+2,1}
, and the forms
f,v\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. There exists
\gamma\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that
\gamma=0
on
bM
,
nd\gamma=nf
,
t\delta\gamma=tv
, and

\|\gamma\|_{W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|f\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|v\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}).

If
M
is only of the class
C^{s+1,1}
, then
\gamma,d\gamma\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

\displaystyle\|\gamma\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|d\gamma\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|f\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|v\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}).

For
C^{1,1}
manifold the condition
t\delta\gamma=v
becomes void.
Moreover, the map from
f
,
v
to
\gamma
is linear.

Proof.

By using a partition of unity, we may assume that the support of
f
and
v
is in one local admissible coordinate chart. Then the boundary condition reads as

\gamma(x^{\prime},0)=0,\quad\frac{\partial\gamma_{I}}{\partial x^{n}}(x^{\prime},0)=f_{nI}(x^{\prime},0),\quad n\notin I,\quad\frac{\partial\gamma_{nI}}{\partial x^{n}}(x^{\prime},0)=-v_{I}(x^{\prime},0).

Here the supports of
f
and
v
belong to
\overline{B}_{R}^{+}
, while
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
is still in coordinate map. Let
\eta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{2R})
,
0\leq\eta\leq 1
, and
\eta=1
on
B_{R}
. Set

\displaystyle\gamma_{I}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta(x)}{2}(U_{1/2}[f_{nI}(\cdot,0)](x)-2U_{1}[f_{nI}(\cdot,0)](x)),\quad n\notin I,

\displaystyle\gamma_{nI}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta(x)}{2}(U_{1/2}[-v_{I}(\cdot,0)](x)-2U_{1}[-v_{I}(\cdot,0)](x)).

By Lemma
3.17
this provides the required extension.
∎

Remark 3.19
.

By Hodge duality, on
C^{s+1,1}
manifold in the situation of Corollary
3.18
we can also get
\gamma\in C^{s+1,1}(M,\Lambda)
with
\delta\gamma\in C^{s+1,1}(M,\Lambda)
and the estimate

\displaystyle\|\gamma\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\delta\gamma\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|f\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|v\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}).

In this case for
C^{1,1}
manifold the condition
nd\gamma=nf
becomes void.

See
[
MS09
]
for the classical Gagliardo type extension operator and its properties.

Chapter 4
Boundary Value Problems for the Hodge Laplacian and Div-Curl Systems — Classical Results

In the Chapter we describe classical boundary value problems for the Hodge Laplacian and div-curl systems and some well-known result on their solvability in constant exponent Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces. However, we emphasize that all these results are valid only when
M
is at least
C^{2,1}.
So we
can not use
these results to treat the case of
C^{1,1}
manifolds.

4.1.
Classical BVPs for the Hodge Laplacian on
C^{2,1}
manifolds

Recall that
\triangle=d\delta+\delta d
and denote

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(f,v)=(df,dv)+(\delta f,\delta v).

From the integration-by-parts formula it follows that

(4.2)

(\triangle f,v)=\mathcal{D}(f,v)+[\delta f,v]-[v,df]

and

\displaystyle(\triangle f,v)-(f,\triangle v)

\displaystyle=[\delta f,v]-[v,df]-[\delta v,f]+[f,dv]

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}=\int\limits_{bM}\langle\nu\wedge\delta f,v\rangle\,d\sigma-\int\limits_{bM}\langle\nu\wedge v,df\rangle\,d\sigma-\int\limits_{bM}\langle\nu\wedge\delta v,f\rangle\,d\sigma\\
+\int\limits_{bM}\langle\nu\wedge f,dv\rangle\,d\sigma.\end{multlined}

From this relation and the definition of
[\cdot,\cdot]
it follows that there are four sets of boundary conditions which make the operator
\triangle
symmetric in
W^{2,2}(M,\Lambda)
:

\displaystyle(I)

\displaystyle\quad t\omega=0,\quad\quad n\omega=0,

\displaystyle(II)

\displaystyle\quad t\omega=0,\quad\quad t\delta\omega=0,

\displaystyle(III)

\displaystyle\quad n\omega=0,\quad\quad nd\omega=0,

\displaystyle(IV)

\displaystyle\quad t\delta\omega=0,\quad nd\omega=0.

These leads to four standard boundary value problems for the Hodge Laplacian:

\displaystyle(I)

\displaystyle\quad\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\omega=t\varphi,\quad n\omega=n\varphi,

\displaystyle(II)

\displaystyle\quad\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\omega=t\varphi,\quad t\delta\omega=t\psi,

\displaystyle(III)

\displaystyle\quad\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad n\omega=n\varphi,\quad nd\omega=n\psi,

\displaystyle(IV)

\displaystyle\quad\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\delta\omega=t\varphi,\quad nd\omega=n\psi.

The first three of these problems are elliptic, moreover the first one has zero kernel/co-kernel, while the fourth one is not elliptic and has infinite dimensional kernel/co-kernel which consists of harmonic fields.

In terms of the classical vector calculus the Green’s relation (
4.2
) takes form

(4.3)

\int\limits_{D}\vec{v}\cdot\triangle\vec{u}\,dV=\int\limits_{\partial D}[(\vec{v}\cdot\vec{n})\mathrm{div}\,\vec{u}-\vec{v}\cdot(\vec{n}\times\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{u})]\,d\sigma-\int\limits_{D}[(\mathrm{div}\,\vec{u})(\mathrm{div}\,\vec{v})+\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{u}\cdot\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{v}]\,dV

(here
\triangle
is the standard Laplacian in 3D) and the four BVPs above read as follows: in a domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
find a vector field
\vec{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
satisfying

\displaystyle(I)

\displaystyle\quad\triangle\vec{u}=\vec{\eta}\quad\text{in}\quad D,\quad\vec{u}=\vec{\varphi}\quad\text{on}\quad\partial D,

\displaystyle(II)

\displaystyle\quad\triangle\vec{u}=\vec{\eta}\quad\text{in}\quad D,\quad\vec{n}\times(\vec{u}-\vec{\varphi})=0,\quad\mathrm{div}\,\vec{u}=\psi\quad\text{on}\quad\partial D,

\displaystyle(III)

\displaystyle\quad\triangle\vec{u}=\vec{\eta}\quad\text{in}\quad D,\quad\vec{n}\cdot(\vec{u}-\vec{\varphi})=0,\quad\vec{n}\times(\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{u}-\vec{\psi})=0\quad\text{on}\quad\partial D,

\displaystyle(IV)

\displaystyle\quad\triangle\vec{u}=\vec{\eta}\quad\text{in}\quad D,\quad\mathrm{div}\,\vec{u}=\varphi,\quad\vec{n}\times(\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{u}-\vec{\psi})=0\quad\text{on}\quad\partial D.

The boundary conditions in (II) and (III) are sometimes called “electric” and “magnetic” boundary conditions, respectively. From (
4.3
) it is easy to get the following representation formula:

(4.4)

\begin{gathered}\vec{u}(x)=\int\limits_{D}g(x,y)\triangle\vec{u}(y)\,dy+\int\limits_{\partial D}\big(\phi_{(n)}(y)\nabla_{y}g(x,y)+g(x,y)\vec{\psi}_{(n)}(y)\big)\,d\sigma(y)\\
+\int\limits_{\partial D}\big(\vec{\psi}_{(t)}(y)\times\nabla_{y}g(x,y)-\vec{n}g(x,y)\phi_{(t)}(y)\big)\,d\sigma(y),\\
g(x,y)=-\frac{1}{4\pi|x-y|},\quad\phi_{(n)}=\vec{u}\cdot\vec{n},\quad\vec{\psi}_{(n)}=[\vec{n}\times\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{u}],\\
\quad\phi_{(t)}=\mathrm{div}\,\vec{u},\quad\vec{\psi}_{(t)}=[\vec{n}\times\vec{u}].\end{gathered}

A solution to the problem (II) or (III) is then sought as the sum of the volume integral and the boundary integral with
\phi_{(t)}
,
\vec{\psi}_{(t)}
or
\phi_{(n)}
,
\vec{\psi}_{(n)}
, respectively, with unknown potentials. Using jump relations for potentials one reduces the problem to boundary integral equations. The three-dimensional boundary value problems (II) and (III), including exterior and interior cases, are analyzed in
[
Kre70
]
by reducing the problem to the boundary integral equations for single- and double-layer potentials. For the tensorial case this analysis was later written in detail by J. Bolik in
[
Bol96
]
and subsequent works (though of course in essence this approach goes back to
[
DS51
]
,
[
DS52
]
). Here in the Euclidian setting to get an analogue if (
4.4
) one uses the Green’s second identity for the Hodge Laplace for the form
u
and the double form
g(x,y)=\Gamma(x-y)\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}(r)}dx^{I}dy^{I}
, with
\Gamma
being the fundamental solution of the standard Laplace operator. In the case of the Riemannian manifold a great role is played by the explicit global construction of the parametrix for the Hodge-Laplacian, see
[
dR84
, Chapter V, §28]
. In variable exponent setting, though, the method of boundary integral equations still is not fully developed since it requires the description of the trace spaces and in the case of rough surfaces estimates of singular integral operators. To carry over the results of
[
MMMT16
]
for rough domains to the variable exponent setting presents a challenging problem.

4.2.
Variational formulations

On
C^{1,1}
manifolds one can not understand the boundary values problems for the Hodge Laplacian in the standard sense. However, these problems are well defined in the variational sense. The four sets of boundary conditions described above correspond to natural variational problems. Consider the equation

(4.5)

(d\omega-\varphi,d\zeta)+(\delta\omega-\psi,\delta\zeta)-(\eta,\zeta)=0

(I)

for
\omega\in W_{0}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
and all
\zeta\in W_{0}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
in the case of boundary conditions
t\omega=0
,
n\omega=0
.

(II)

for
\omega\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
and all
\zeta\in W_{T}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
for the case of Dirichlet boundary condition;

(III)

for
\omega\in W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
and all
\zeta\in W_{N}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
for the case of Neumann boundary condition;

(IV)

for
\omega\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
and all
\zeta\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
for a manifold without boundary or for the case of “natural” boundary conditions
t\delta\omega=0
,
nd\omega=0
.

If
\eta,\varphi,\psi\in L^{2}(M,\Lambda)
, equation (
4.5
) is the Euler-Lagrange equation for the variational problem

\mathcal{F}[\omega]=\frac{1}{2}(d\omega,d\omega)+\frac{1}{2}(\delta\omega,\delta\omega)-(\varphi,d\omega)-(\psi,\delta\omega)-(\eta,\omega)\rightarrow\mathrm{min}

over

(I)

\omega\in W_{0}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
, here in (
4.5
) one allows
\zeta\in W_{0}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
.

(II)

\omega\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
for the Dirichlet problem, under assumption
\eta\in\mathcal{H}_{T}^{\perp}
, here in (
4.5
) one allows
\zeta\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
;

(III)

or
\omega\in W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
for the Neumann problem, under assumption
\eta\in\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\perp}
, here in (
4.5
) one allows
\zeta\in W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
.

(IV)

\omega\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
for a closed manifold or for “natural” boundary conditions, under assumption
\eta\in(\mathcal{H}(M)\cap L^{2}(M,\Lambda))^{\perp}
, here in (
4.5
) one allows
\zeta\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
.

Since
\mathcal{F}[\omega]=\mathcal{F}[\omega+h]
for (II)
h\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
, (III)
h\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
, (IV)
h\in\mathcal{H}(M)\cap L^{2}(M,\Lambda)
, the set of admissible forms can be reduced to (II)
\omega\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))^{\perp}
, (III)
\omega\in W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{N}(M))^{\perp}
, (IV)
\omega\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}(M)\cap L^{2}(M,\Lambda))^{\perp}
. The existence and uniqueness of the minimizer then follows by the direct method in the calculus of variations. In this the Gaffney inequality (cf. Theorem 7.5.1 in
[
Mor66
]
for
C^{1,1}
manifolds) is instrumental:

\|\omega\|^{2}_{W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)}\leq C_{1}(\omega,\omega)+C_{2}\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega),\quad\omega\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)\cap W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda),

which implies by the standard compactness argument

\displaystyle(\omega,\omega)\leq C\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega),\quad\text{and so}\quad\|\omega\|^{2}_{W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)}\leq C\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega),\quad\text{for}

\displaystyle\omega\in W_{0}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda),\quad\omega\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))^{\perp},\quad\omega\in W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{N}(M))^{\perp}.

Here one also uses the well-known result on the triviality of the set of harmonic forms in
W_{0}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
. Using the Hodge decomposition one can also obtain similar estimates for
\omega\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}(M)\cap L^{2}(M,\Lambda))^{\perp}
(for instance, Lemma
6.18
below).

We shall be mainly interested in the Dirichlet and Neumann problems. From the classical results of
[
Mor66
]
it follows that in these cases solutions to (
4.5
) belong to
W^{2,2}(M,\Lambda)
as soon as
\eta\in L^{2}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
and
M
is
C^{2,1}
. The integration by parts in (
4.5
) yields then the equation and boundary conditions.

On
C^{2,1}
manifolds the variational theory of standard boundary value problems for the Hodge Laplacian can be described by the following statements.

•

The minimizer of
\mathcal{F[\omega]}
over the space of
W^{1,2}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
is in
W^{2,2}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfies

\triangle\omega=\eta+\delta\varphi+d\psi\quad\text{a.e. in $M$},\quad t\omega=0,\quad n\omega=0.

•

The minimizer of
\mathcal{F[\omega]}
over the space of
(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))^{\perp}\cap W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
belongs to
W^{2,2}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfies

\triangle\omega=\eta+\delta\varphi+d\psi\quad\text{a.e. in $M$},\quad t\omega=0,\quad t\delta\omega=t\psi.

•

The minimizer of
\mathcal{F[\omega]}
over the space of
(\mathcal{H}_{N}(M))^{\perp}\cap W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
belongs to
W^{2,2}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfies

\triangle\omega=\eta+\delta\varphi+d\psi\quad\text{a.e. in $M$},\quad n\omega=0,\quad nd\omega=n\varphi.

•

The minimizer of
\mathcal{F[\omega]}
over the space of
(\mathcal{H}(M)\cap L^{2}(M,\Lambda))^{\perp}\cap W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
belongs to
W^{2,2}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfies

\triangle\omega=\eta+\delta\varphi+d\psi\quad\text{a.e. in $M$},\quad t\delta\omega=t\psi,\quad nd\omega=n\varphi.

Another classical approach based on the jump relations and boundary integral equations was developed by Duff, Spencer and later extended by R. Kress, J. Bolik and some other authors.

4.3.
Dirichlet and Neumann potentials

If
\eta\in L^{2}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)^{\perp}
then the Dirichlet potential
G_{D}[\eta]
is a unique minimizer of the variational problem

(4.6)

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\frac{1}{2}(d\omega,d\omega)+\frac{1}{2}(\delta\omega,\delta\omega)-(\eta,\omega):\omega\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))^{\perp}\right\}.

By the Gaffney inequality (cf. Theorem 7.5.1 in
[
Mor66
]
), which holds for
C^{1,1}
manifolds, it is easy to prove by standard variational methods that the minimizer exists and is unique. We define this unique minimizer as the Dirichlet potential of
\eta
and denote this by
G_{D}[\eta]
. The Dirichlet potential
\omega=G_{D}[\eta]
belongs to
W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))^{\perp}
and satisfies the variational relation

(4.7)

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\xi)=(\eta,\xi)\quad\text{for all}\quad\xi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda).

On the other hand, for
\eta\in L^{2}(M,\Lambda)
any solution
\omega\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)^{\perp}
of this relation minimizes the variational problem (
4.6
).

The variational relation (
4.7
) is well defined for
\eta\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
and
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
. By regularity results of
[
Mor66
]
any
\omega\in W_{T}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfying (
4.7
) with
\eta=0
belongs to
W^{1,q}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
for any
q<\infty
. Using approximation it is easy to show that
\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega)=0
, thus
\omega\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
, which implies
\omega=0
.

Definition 4.1

(Dirichlet potential)
.

For
\eta\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfying
(\eta,h_{T})=0
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
by
G_{D}[\eta]\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
we denote a (unique) solution of (
4.7
) satisfying
(\omega,h_{T})=0
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
.

An existence result for the Dirichlet potentials will be provided in Section
5
.

Similarly, for
\eta\in L^{2}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{N}(M))^{\perp}
the Neumann potential
G_{N}[\eta]
is a unique minimizer of the variational problem

(4.8)

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\frac{1}{2}(d\omega,d\omega)+\frac{1}{2}(\delta\omega,\delta\omega)-(\eta,\omega):\omega\in W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{N}(M))^{\perp}\right\}.

Its existence is proved in the same way as for the Dirichlet potential. By construction, it belongs to

W^{1,2}_{N}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{N}(M))^{\perp}
and satisfies the variational relation

(4.9)

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\xi)=(\eta,\xi)\quad\text{for all}\quad\xi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda).

Definition 4.2

(Neumann potential)
.

For
\eta\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfying
(\eta,h_{N})=0
for all
h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
by
G_{N}[\eta]\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
we denote a (unique) solution of (
4.9
) satisfying
(\omega,h_{N})=0
for all
h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
.

An existence result for the Neumann potentials will be provided in Section
5
.

Remark 4.3
.

Note that as soon as the manifold is at least
C^{2,1}
, the Dirichlet potential
G_{D}[\eta]
can be equivalently defined as the
W^{2,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
solution of

\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\omega=0.\quad t\delta\omega=0,

satisfying
\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega=0
. Similarly, assuming the manifold to be at least
C^{2,1}
, the Neumann potential
G_{N}[\eta]
can be equivalently defined as the
W^{2,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
solution of the problem

\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad n\omega=0,\quad nd\omega=0,

satisfying
\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega=0
.

4.4.
Potentials for full Dirichlet data

We can also consider the following problem:

(4.10)

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\frac{1}{2}(d\omega,d\omega)+\frac{1}{2}(\delta\omega,\delta\omega)-(\eta,\omega):\omega\in W^{1,2}_{0}(M,\Lambda)\right\}.

Due to Gaffney’s inequality and triviality of the set of harmonic fields vanishing on
bM
(this again only requires that the manifold
M
be
C^{1,1}
, see
[
AKS62
]
) this problem has a unique minimizer in
W^{1,2}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
, which we denote by
G_{0}[\eta]
. This minimizer satisfies

(4.11)

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\xi)=(\eta,\xi)\quad\text{for all}\quad\xi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda).

Definition 4.4

(Potential for full Dirichlet data)
.

For
\eta\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
by
G_{0}[\eta]\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
we denote a (unique) solution of (
4.11
).

As soon as
M
is
C^{2,1}
, the potential
G_{0}[\eta]
can be equivalently defined as a unique
W^{2,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
solution of the problem

\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\omega=0,\quad n\omega=0.

4.5.
Results from general elliptic theory

Let
M
be a compact Riemanninan manifold with boundary of the class
C^{s+2,1}
. From the general elliptic theory it follows that (see for instance
[
Mor66
,
Sch95
]
, also
[
Sil17
]
for a direct proof which does not rely on results about general elliptic problems) that both Dirichlet and Neumann boundary value problems

(4.12)

\displaystyle\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\omega=t\varphi,\quad t\delta\omega=t\psi,

(4.13)

\displaystyle\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad n\omega=n\varphi,\quad nd\omega=n\psi,

are Fredholm solvable, possess the finite-dimensional kernels consisting of Dirichlet fields
\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
and Neumann fields
\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
respectively, a solution satisfies the general elliptic estimate

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{W^{s+2,p}(M,\Lambda)}\lesssim\|\eta\|_{W^{s,p}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\varphi\|_{W^{s+2-1/p,p}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\psi\|_{W^{s+1-1/p,p}(M,\Lambda)}

(4.14)

\displaystyle+\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(M,\Lambda)},

where
s\geq 0
and
p>1
. The problem (
4.12
) is solvable if and only if
(\eta,h_{T})=[\psi,h_{T}]
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
. The problem (
4.13
) is solvable if and only if
(\eta,h_{T})=-[h_{N},\psi]
for all
h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
.

The last term on the RHS of (
4.14
) can be removed if one considers
\omega
satisfying
(\omega,\mathcal{H}_{*}(M))=0
, or more generally consider
\omega-\mathcal{P}_{*}\omega
where
\mathcal{P}_{*}
is a projector on
\mathcal{H}_{*}(M)
(further
\mathcal{H}_{*}(M)=\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
for the Dirichlet problem and
\mathcal{H}_{*}(M)=\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
for the Neumann problem):

(4.15)

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{*}\omega\|_{W^{s+2,p}(M,\Lambda)}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{W^{s,p}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\varphi\|_{W^{s+2-1/p,p}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\psi\|_{W^{s+1-1/p,p}(M,\Lambda)}),

but the constant in (
4.15
) comes from a functional analytic argument, and so is not quantitative. We shall use only the standard projectors
\mathcal{P}_{T}
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}
defined by (
2.18
), (
2.17
).

4.6.
First order systems

Dirichlet condition.
Consider the following problem: find
\omega\in W^{1,p}(M,\Lambda)
,
p>1
, satisfying

(4.16)

d\omega=f\in L^{p}(M,\Lambda),\quad\delta\omega=v\in L^{p}(M,\Lambda),\quad t\omega=t\varphi,\quad\varphi\in W^{1,p}(M,\Lambda).

The problem (
4.16
) is solvable if and only if the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle df=0,\quad t(f-d\varphi)=0,\quad(f,h_{T})=[\varphi,h_{T}]\quad\forall h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M),

\displaystyle\delta v=0,\quad(v,h_{T})=0\quad\forall h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M).

Neumann condition.
Consider the following problem: find
\omega\in W^{1,p}(M)
satisfying

(4.17)

d\omega=f\in L^{p}(M,\Lambda),\quad\delta\omega=v\in L^{p}(M,\Lambda),\quad n\omega=n\varphi,\quad\varphi\in W^{1,p}(M,\Lambda).

The problem (
4.17
) is solvable if and only if the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle\delta v=0,\quad n(v-\delta\varphi)=0,\quad(v,h_{N})=-[h_{N},\varphi]\quad\forall h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M),

\displaystyle df=0,\quad(f,h_{N})=0\quad\forall h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M).

In both cases (
4.16
), (
4.17
), a solution can be chosen so that
[
Sch95
]
,
[
Sil17
]
for
s\geq 0
and
p>1
there holds

\|\omega\|_{W^{s+1,p}(M,\Lambda)}\leq C(\|f\|_{W^{s,p}(M,\Lambda)}+\|v\|_{W^{s,p}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\varphi\|_{W^{s+1-1/p,p}(bM,\Lambda)}),

but the constant is generally (without presence of some norm of
\omega
on the right) not quantitative. Any solution satisfies the general elliptic a priori estimate

\|\omega\|_{W^{s+1,p}(M,\Lambda)}\\
\leq C(\|f\|_{W^{s,p}(M,\Lambda)}+\|v\|_{W^{s,p}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\varphi\|_{W^{s+1-1/p,p}(bM,\Lambda)}+\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(M,\Lambda)}),

with a
quantitative
constant.

In terms of the classical vector calculus the problems (
4.16
), (
4.17
) read as follows: in a domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
find a vector field
\vec{u}
with given rotor and divergence

\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{u}=\vec{f},\quad\mathrm{div}\,\vec{u}=\vec{v}\quad\text{in}\quad D

and given tangential component
\vec{n}\times\vec{u}=\vec{n}\times\vec{\varphi}
or normal component
\vec{n}\cdot\vec{u}=\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\varphi}
, respectively, on
\partial D
. For
3D
systems from (
4.3
) one easily gets the following representation formula

\displaystyle\vec{u}(x)=-\int\limits_{D}((\mathrm{div}\,\vec{u})(y)\nabla_{y}g(x,y)+[\mathrm{rot}\,\vec{u}(y)\times\nabla_{y}g(x,y)])\,dy

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\partial D}(\phi(y)\nabla_{y}g(x,y)+[\vec{\psi}(y)\times\nabla_{y}g(x,y)])\,d\sigma(y),

where
\phi(y)=\vec{u}\cdot\vec{n}
,
\vec{\psi}=\vec{n}\times\vec{u}
,
g(x,y)=-(4\pi|x-y|)^{-1}
. The solution is then sought as the sum of the volume integrals in this representation and the third/fourth integral with an unknown density. To pass to the boundary integral equation it remains to recall the jump relations for derivatives of the single layer potential and for the double layer potential. First-order div-curl type systems for alternating tensor fields were treated in
[
Kre70
]
in the Euclidian case using the method of boundary integral equations (in Hölder spaces). Later this approach was expanded by J. Bolik to incorporate theory in Sobolev spaces (with constant exponent). In three-dimensional variable exponent setting certain estimates for the div-curl system were obtained in
[
SR20
]
by the boundary integral equations method. However in the main estimate of this work the constant is non-qualified since it comes from the functional-analytic argument (essentially the Fredholm alternative and the fact that the homogeneous boundary integral equation has only trivial solution due to the assumptions on the domain).

Chapter 5
The Dirichlet and Neumann Problem in Variable Exponent Spaces

In this Chapter we consider the Dirichlet and Neumann problems for the Hodge Laplacian in variable exponent spaces. Let
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and let the manifold
M
be at least of the class
C^{1,1}
. We shall also consider two other classical variants of boundary conditions for the Hodge Laplacian: namely with
t\omega=0
and
n\omega=0
and with
t\delta\omega=0
,
nd\omega=0
, with the latter one postponed to Section
6
.

Recall the notation
(\cdot,\cdot)
,
[\cdot,\cdot]
and
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,\cdot)
introduced in (
2.5
), (
2.7
), and (
4.1
), respectively, and the notation
\|\cdot\|_{p(\cdot),M}
,
\|\cdot\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}
for the norms in
L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and in
W^{k,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, respectively. We also recall the main variational relation (
4.5
):

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)=(\eta,\zeta)+(\varphi,d\zeta)+(\psi,\delta\zeta)

for
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
,
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
, where
*\in\{0,T,N\}
. We present now the results of Section
7
which provide the basis for subsequent investigations.

Theorem

(Theorems
7.13
,
7.23
)
.

Let
M
be of the class
C^{1,1}
. Assume
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
,
*\in\{0,T,N\}
satisfy (
4.5
) where
\varphi,\psi,\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
for any
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

If
M
is of the class
C^{s+2,1}
for
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\eta\in W^{s.p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

For
C^{1,1}
manifolds we shall also use the following results:

Theorem

(Theorem
7.24
)
.

Let
\omega_{0},d\omega_{0}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda).
Suppose
\omega\in\omega_{0}+W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy
t\alpha=t\psi
,
t\beta=td\omega_{0}
,

(5.1)

d\alpha+\delta\beta=\eta+d\psi+\delta\varphi,

and

(5.2)

\mathcal{D}(\alpha,\zeta)=(\eta+d\psi,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(\beta,\zeta)=(\eta+\delta\varphi,\delta\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
.

Theorem

(Theorem
7.26
)
.

Let
\omega_{0},\delta\omega_{0}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda).
Suppose
\omega\in\omega_{0}+W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy
n\alpha=n\delta\omega_{0}
,
n\beta=n\varphi
, (
5.1
), and (
5.2
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
.

Theorem

(Theorem
7.29
)
.

Let
\omega_{0},d\omega_{0},\delta\omega_{0}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda).
Suppose
\omega\in\omega_{0}+W_{0}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy
n\alpha=n\delta\omega_{0}
,
n\beta=nd\omega_{0}
, (
5.1
), and (
5.2
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
.

5.1.
The Dirichlet problem for the Hodge Laplacian

Our main result in this Section — Theorem
5.4
— concerns the solvability of the boundary value problem

(5.3)

\triangle\omega=\eta\quad\text{in}\quad M,\quad t\omega=t\varphi,\quad t\delta\omega=t\psi\quad\text{on}\quad bM

in variable exponent Sobolev spaces. When
M
is at least
C^{s+2,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, we solve this problem in
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, with the equation understood in the a.e. sense and the boundary conditions in the sense of trace. When
M
is only
C^{1,1}
, a weak solution
\omega
to (
5.3
) is understood as a form
\omega\in\varphi+W_{T}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfying the following integral identity:

(5.4)

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)-(\eta-d\psi,\zeta)-(\psi,\delta\zeta)=0

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
.

The central result of this Section is Theorem
5.4
. To prove this theorem we require several auxiliary results. First, we state a regularity result of Lemma
5.1
, which is a partial case of Theorems
7.13
,
7.23
. Next, we state and prove Lemma
5.2
which allows to get rid of the
\|\omega\|_{1,M}
term on the right-hand side of the a priori estimate of Lemma
5.1
. Combining these results we finally prove Theorem
5.4
. A partial case of Theorem
5.4
— an existence and regularity result for the Dirichlet potential — is stated in Theorem
5.5
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
be a solution of (
5.4
).

(1)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{1,1}
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and there exists a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0
such that

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

(2)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+2,1}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be given. Then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfies (
5.3
) and we have the estimate

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

Proof.

The form
\omega^{\prime}=\omega-\varphi\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
satisfies

\mathcal{D}(\omega^{\prime},\zeta)=(\eta-d\psi,\zeta)+(\psi-d\varphi,d\zeta)+(-\delta\varphi,\delta\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. It remains to use Theorem
7.23
for (a) and Theorem
7.13
for (b). If
\omega\in W^{2,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
then by the integration-by-parts formula we get the first and third relations in (
5.3
).
∎

The constant in Lemma
5.1
is quantitative. The constant which comes from the general elliptic results for estimates without the norm on the RHS (see (
4.15
)) is generally non-quantitative. But it can be estimated via the constant in the following standard inequality for
\omega\in W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
orthogonal to
\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
:

(5.5)

\|\omega\|_{2,M}^{2}\leq C_{T}\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega)=C_{T}(\|d\omega\|_{2,M}^{2}+\|\delta\omega\|_{2,M}^{2}).

The best such constant
C_{T}
is nothing else as the reciprocal of the first positive eigenvalue of the Hodge Laplacian on
M
with the Dirichlet boundary conditions
t\omega=0
,
t\delta\omega=0
. For the constant
C_{T}
for a ball
B_{R}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, an easy scaling argument shows
C_{T}(B_{R})=C(n)R^{2}
. For more on the constant, see
[
CDK12
]
,
[
Csa13
]
,
[
CDS18
]
, also the works in spectral geometry
[
GS03
,
RS11
]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\eta,\varphi,\psi\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta=0
. Let
\omega\in W_{T}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(p_{-},M)
such that

(5.6)

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega\|_{p_{-},M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\varphi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\psi\|_{p_{-},M}).

Proof.

Assume without loss that
p_{-}\leq 2
. Let
\mu\in L^{p_{-}^{\prime}}(M,\Lambda)
and
\xi=G_{D}[\mu-\mathcal{P}_{T}\mu]\in W^{1,p_{-}^{\prime}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. There holds

(5.7)

\begin{gathered}(\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega,\mu)=(\mu-\mathcal{P}_{T}\mu,\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega)=\mathcal{D}(\xi,\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega)\\
=\mathcal{D}(\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega,\xi)=(\eta,\xi)+(\varphi,d\xi)+(\psi,\delta\xi).\end{gathered}

From (
5.5
) it follows that

\displaystyle\|\xi\|_{2,M}^{2}\leq C_{T}\mathcal{D}(\xi,\xi)=C_{T}(\mu-\mathcal{P}_{T}\mu,\xi)\leq C_{T}\|\mu\|_{2,M}\|\xi\|_{2,M}.

Then

\|\xi\|_{2,M}\leq C_{T}\|\mu\|_{2,M}.

The
quantitative
elliptic estimate of Lemma
5.1
for
\xi
and the Hölder inequality now give

\|\xi\|_{1,p_{-}^{\prime},M}\leq C\|\mu\|_{p_{-}^{\prime},M}

with the constant
C
which depends on the data and the constant
C_{T}
in (
5.5
). Then from (
5.7
) we get (
5.6
).
∎

Combining the results of Lemmas
5.1
,
5.2
we get the following

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
be a solution of (
5.4
) and
\mathcal{P}_{T}(\omega-\varphi)=0
.

(1)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{1,1}
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and there exists a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0
such that

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}).

(2)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+2,1}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be given. Then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfies (
5.3
) and we have the estimate

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}).

Proof.

We repeat the proof of Lemma
5.1
. Now for
\omega^{\prime}=\omega-\varphi
by assumption we have
\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega^{\prime}=0
. Thus by Lemma
5.2
we can estimate

\displaystyle\|\omega^{\prime}\|_{p_{-},M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta-d\psi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\psi-d\varphi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\delta\varphi\|_{p_{-},M})

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}).

It remains to use again Theorem
7.13
for (b) and Theorem
7.23
for (a).
∎

The central result of this Section is

Theorem 5.4
.

Consider the boundary value problem (
5.3
).

(1)

Let
M
be
C^{1,1}
and let
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
(\eta,h_{T})=[\psi,h_{T}]
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M).
Then there exists a solution
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
of (
5.3
) understood in the sense of (
5.4
) such that for some constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0,
we have

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta-d\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}).

Moreover, if
d\varphi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, satisfy

(5.8)

\begin{gathered}d\alpha+\delta\beta=\eta,\quad t\alpha=t\psi,\quad t\beta=td\varphi,\\
\mathcal{D}(\alpha,\zeta)=(\eta,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(\beta,\zeta)=(\eta-d\psi,\delta\zeta)\end{gathered}

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
and there holds

(5.9)

\begin{gathered}\|\alpha\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta-d\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M)}),\\
\|\beta\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta-d\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|d\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}).\end{gathered}

(2)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+2,1}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be such that
(\eta,h_{T})=[\psi,h_{T}]
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
. Then there exists a solution
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of the boundary value problem (
5.3
)
such that for some constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)>0,
we have

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).

Moreover, the potentials
\alpha=\delta\omega
,
\beta=d\omega
satisfy (
5.8
) and there holds

(5.10)

\begin{gathered}\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta-d\psi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}),\\
\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta-d\psi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|d\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).\end{gathered}

Proof.

(a) We approximate
\eta
and
\varphi
,
\psi
by Lipschitz forms
\eta_{\varepsilon}
,
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
,
\psi_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, correspondingly. Let
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\in W_{T}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))^{\perp}
be the (unique) minimizer of the functional

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}(\omega^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})-((\eta_{\varepsilon}-d\psi_{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{P}_{T}(\eta_{\varepsilon}-d\psi_{\varepsilon}),\omega^{\prime})-(\psi_{\varepsilon}-d\varphi_{\varepsilon},d\omega^{\prime})-(-\delta\varphi_{\varepsilon},\delta\omega^{\prime})

over
W_{T}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))^{\perp}
. The existence is guaranteed by the Gaffney inequality and the direct method of the calculus of variations (see Section
4.2
and
[
Mor66
]
). Then
\omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
and
\omega_{\varepsilon}=\omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}+\varphi_{\varepsilon}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\zeta)=((\eta_{\varepsilon}-d\psi_{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{P}_{T}(\eta_{\varepsilon}-d\psi_{\varepsilon}),\zeta)+(\psi_{\varepsilon}-d\varphi_{\varepsilon},d\zeta)+(-\delta\varphi_{\varepsilon},\delta\zeta),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\omega_{\varepsilon},\zeta)=((\eta_{\varepsilon}-d\psi_{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{P}_{T}(\eta_{\varepsilon}-d\psi_{\varepsilon}),\zeta)+(\psi_{\varepsilon},d\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
, respectively. By Corollary
5.3
,

\displaystyle\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M})

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M})

and the sequence
\omega_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. By the same estimates applied to the difference
\omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\omega_{\varepsilon_{2}}\in(\varphi_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\varphi_{\varepsilon_{2}})+W^{1,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
, the sequence
\omega_{\varepsilon}
converges in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Passing to the limit in
\varepsilon\to 0
and using that
\mathcal{P}_{T}(\eta_{\varepsilon}-d\psi_{\varepsilon})
converges to
\mathcal{P}_{T}(\eta-d\psi)=0
in
L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
we get the existence of a limit
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
which satisfies the integral identity (
5.4
) and thus is the required solution of the boundary value problem (
5.3
).

Then by Theorem
7.24
for
\alpha=\delta\omega
,
\beta=d\omega
we have
\alpha,\beta\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and (
5.8
). Clearly,

(5.11)

\mathcal{D}(\alpha-\psi,\zeta)=(\eta-d\psi,d\zeta)+(-\delta\psi,\delta\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(\beta-d\varphi)=(\eta-d\psi-\delta(d\varphi),\delta\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. The estimate (
5.9
) follow then by Theorem
7.23
and Lemma
5.2
using that
\mathcal{P}_{T}(\beta-d\varphi)=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{T}(\alpha-\psi)=-\mathcal{P}_{T}\psi
.

(b) We argue as in (a). In this case by Corollary
5.3
we get

\displaystyle\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M})

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M})

Thus
\omega_{\varepsilon}
is a bounded sequence in
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and by a similar estimate applied to
\omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\omega_{\varepsilon_{2}}
we get the convergence of
\omega_{\varepsilon}
in
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. In this case, since
\omega
is at least
W^{2,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
, the direct integration-by-parts in (
5.4
) yields (
5.3
), and we do not need a recourse to Theorem
7.24
to establish the required relations (
5.8
) for
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
. It remains to use Theorem
7.13
and Lemma
5.2
for relations (
5.11
) to obtain estimates (
5.10
)

∎

We state a partial case of the previous theorem as a separate result for Dirichlet potentials. Recall that on
C^{2,1}
manifold for
\eta\in L^{p{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta=0
, by
G_{D}[\eta]
(the Dirichlet potential of
\eta
) we denote a unique solution
\omega\in W^{2,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
of

\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\omega=0,\quad t\delta\omega=0,\quad\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega=0.

On
C^{1,1}
manifold we understand
G_{D}[\eta]
as the unique
W^{1,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
solution of

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)=(\eta,\zeta)\quad\text{for all}\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)

satisfying
\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega=0
. On
C^{2,1}
manifold these two notions are equivalent.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
M
be
C^{1,1}
and let
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta=0
.

(1)

There exist a unique Dirichlet potential
\omega:=G_{D}[\eta]\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0
such that

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}.

Moreover,
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\eta]
and
\beta=dG_{D}[\eta]
belong to
W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, satisfy

(5.12)

t\alpha=0,\quad t\beta=0,\quad\eta=d\alpha+\delta\beta,

along with the estimate

(5.13)

\|\alpha\|_{1,p(\cdot),M},\|\beta\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}.

and the relations

(5.14)

\mathcal{D}(\alpha,\zeta)=(\eta,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(\beta,\zeta)=(\eta,\delta\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
.

(2)

Furthermore, if
M
is
C^{s+2,1}
and
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
with
s\geq 0
integer, then
\omega:=G_{D}[\eta]\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and there exists a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)>0
such that

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

Moreover,
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\eta]
and
\beta=dG_{D}[\eta]
belong to
W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, satisfy (
5.12
), (
5.14
), and the estimate

\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M},\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

Corollary 5.6
.

If
\eta\in W^{\delta,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
then
\delta G_{D}[\eta-\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta]=G_{D}[\delta\eta]
and if
\eta\in W_{T}^{d,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
then
dG_{D}[\eta-\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta]=G_{D}[d\eta]
.

Proof.

If
\delta\eta
is defined and at least in
L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
(clearly this condition is nontrivial only if
s=0
) then again using the integration-by-parts formula we see that the relation in (
5.14
) for
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\eta-\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta]
coincides with the relation defining
G_{D}[\delta\eta]
. Since
t\alpha=0
and
(\alpha,h_{T})=0
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
, we immediately arrive at
\alpha=G_{D}[\delta\eta]
. The same reasoning for
\beta=dG_{D}[\eta-\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta]
yields
\beta=G_{D}[d\eta]
under the additional assumption
t\eta=0
.
∎

5.2.
The Neumann problem for the Hodge Laplacian

In this Section we consider the boundary value problem

(5.15)

\triangle\omega=\eta\quad\text{in}\quad M,\quad n\omega=n\varphi,\quad nd\omega=n\psi\quad\text{on}\quad bM.

If
M
is at least
C^{s+2,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
then we solve this problem in
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. If
M
is only
C^{1,1}
then a weak solution to (
5.15
) is understood as
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
satisfying

(5.16)

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)-(\eta-\delta\psi,\zeta)-(\psi,d\zeta)=0

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p_{-}}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
be a solution of (
5.16
).

(1)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{1,1}
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and there exists a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0
such that

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

(2)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+2,1}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be given. Then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfies (
5.15
) and we have the estimate

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

For
\omega\in W_{N}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
such that
\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega=0
there exists a constant
C_{N}=C_{N}(M)
such that

\|\omega\|_{2,M}^{2}\leq C_{N}\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega).

Recall that if
M
is at least
C^{2,1}
for
\eta\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}\eta=0
, by
G_{N}[\eta]
(the Neumann potential of
\eta
) we denote a unique solution
\omega\in W^{2,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
of

\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad n\omega=0,\quad nd\omega=0,\quad\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega=0.

On
C^{1,1}
manifold we understand
G_{N}[\eta]
as the unique
W^{1,p_{-}}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
solution of

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)=(\eta,\zeta)\quad\text{for all}\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda),

satisfying
\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega=0
. On
C^{2,1}
manifold these two notions coincide.

The following statements are proved in the same way as for the Dirichlet boundary conditions or can be obtained by duality.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\eta,\varphi,\psi\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}\eta=0
. Let
\omega\in W_{N}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(p_{-},M)
such that

(5.17)

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega\|_{p_{-},M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\varphi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\psi\|_{p_{-},M}).

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p_{-}}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
be a solution of (
5.16
) and
\mathcal{P}_{T}(\omega-\varphi)=0
.

(1)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{1,1}
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and there exists a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0
such that

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}).

(2)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+2,1}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be given. Then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfies (
5.15
) and we have the estimate

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}).

Theorem 5.10
.

Consider the boundary value problem (
5.15
).

(1)

Let
M
be
C^{1,1}
and let
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
(\eta,h_{N})=-[\psi,h_{N}]
for all
h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M).
Then there exists a solution
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
of (
5.15
) understood in the sense of (
5.16
) such that for some constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0
we have

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta-d\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}).

Moreover, if
\delta\varphi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, satisfy

(5.18)

\begin{gathered}d\alpha+\delta\beta=\eta,\quad n\alpha=n\delta\varphi,\quad n\beta=n\psi,\\
\mathcal{D}(\alpha,\zeta)=(\eta-\delta\psi,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(\beta,\zeta)=(\eta,\delta\zeta)\end{gathered}

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
and there holds

(5.19)

\begin{gathered}\|\alpha\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta-\delta\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}),\\
\|\beta\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta-\delta\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}).\end{gathered}

(2)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+2,1}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be such that
(\eta,h_{N})=-[\psi,h_{N}]
for all
h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
. Then there exists a solution
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of the boundary value problem (
5.15
)
such that for some constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)>0,
we have

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).

Moreover, the potentials
\alpha=\delta\omega
,
\beta=d\omega
satisfy (
5.18
) and there holds

(5.20)

\begin{gathered}\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta-\delta\psi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}),\\
\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta-\delta\psi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).\end{gathered}

We state a partial case of this theorem as a separate result for Neumann potentials.

Theorem 5.11
.

(i) Let
M
be a
C^{s+2,1}
manifold. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta=0
. Then there exist a unique Neumann potential
\omega:=G_{N}[\eta]\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of
\eta
satisfying

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}

where
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
. Moreover, we also have that
\alpha=\delta G_{N}[\eta]
and
\beta=dG_{N}[\eta]
belong to
W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, satisfy

(5.21)

n\alpha=0,\quad n\beta=0,\quad\eta=d\alpha+\delta\beta,

the estimate

\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M},\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

and the relations

(5.22)

\mathcal{D}(\alpha,\zeta)=(\eta,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(\beta,\zeta)=(\eta,\delta\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
.

(ii) If
s=0
and
M
is only
C^{1,1}
then
G_{N}[\eta]\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfies

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}.

Moreover,
\alpha=\delta G_{N}[\eta]
and
\beta=dG_{N}[\eta]
belong to
W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, satisfy (
5.22
), (
5.21
) and

\|\alpha\|_{1,p(\cdot),M},\|\beta\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}.

Corollary 5.12
.

If
\eta\in W^{\delta,p_{-}}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
then
\delta G_{N}[\eta-\mathcal{P}_{N}\eta]=G_{N}[\delta\eta]
and if
\eta\in W^{d,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
then
dG_{N}[\eta-\mathcal{P}_{N}\eta]=G_{N}[d\eta]
.

5.3.
Full Dirichlet data

In this Section we consider the boundary value problem

(5.23)

\triangle\omega=\eta\quad\text{in}\quad M,\quad\omega=\varphi\quad\text{on}\quad bM

in variable exponent Sobolev spaces. When
M
is at least
C^{s+2,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, we solve this problem in
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, with the equation understood in the a.e. sense and the boundary conditions in the sense of trace. When
M
is only
C^{1,1}
, a weak solution
\omega
to (
5.23
) is understood as a form
\omega\in\varphi+W_{0}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfying the following integral identity:

(5.24)

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)-(\eta,\zeta)=0

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
.

Lemma 5.13
.

(i) Let
M
be
C^{s+2,1}
. Assume
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be given. Let
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p_{-}}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
5.24
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, there holds

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}),

and
\omega
satisfies (
5.23
).

(ii) If
M
is of the class
C^{1,1}
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, then
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
and there holds

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M})

It is well known (see, for instance
[
Mor66
]
) that
\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)\cap\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)=\{0\}
, so the problem (
5.24
) has a unique solution in
W^{1,2}_{0}(M)
for all
\eta\in L^{2}(M)
and for all
\omega\in W_{0}^{1,2}(M)
there holds

(\omega,\omega)\leq C_{0}\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega),

where
C_{0}
is the reciprocal of the first eigenvalue of the Hodge Laplacian with boundary conditions
t\omega=0
,
n\omega=0
. Further for
\eta\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
we denote the solution
\omega\in W_{0}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
to (
5.24
) by
G_{0}[\eta]
.

The same arguments as before give the following results.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
\eta,\varphi,\psi\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
. Let
\omega\in W_{0}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(p_{-},M)
such that

(5.25)

\|\omega\|_{p_{-},M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\varphi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\psi\|_{p_{-},M}).

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p_{-}}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
be a solution of (
5.24
).

(1)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{1,1}
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and there exists a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0
such that

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}).

(2)

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+2,1}
. Suppose
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be given. Then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfies (
5.23
) and we have the estimate

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}).

Theorem 5.16
.

Consider the boundary value problem (
5.23
).

(1)

Let
M
be
C^{1,1}
and let
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\varphi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists a solution
\omega\in\varphi+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
of (
5.23
) understood in the sense of (
5.24
) such that for some constant
C=C(\mathrm{data})>0
we have
