\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}).

The potentials
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy

d\alpha+\delta\beta=\eta
.

(2)

Let
M
be
C^{s+2,1}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists a solution
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of the boundary value problem (
5.23
)
such that for some constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)>0,
we have

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}).

5.4.
Hodge decomposition in variable exponent spaces

The following theorems are standard Hodge decomposition results stated in variable exponent setting. The proofs are fairly standard and included here only for the reader’s convenience. Let
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, and
M
be of the class
C^{s+1,1}
. In the following statements
C
is a quantitative constant which depends only on
p_{-}
,
p_{+}
,
c_{\mathrm{log}}(p)
,
M
, and
s
.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
\omega\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. There exist
\alpha,\beta\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
h\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
such that

\displaystyle\omega=h+d\alpha+\delta\beta,

\displaystyle t\alpha=0,\quad\delta\alpha=0,\quad t\beta=0,\quad d\beta=0,

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M},\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

These forms are given by
h=\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega
,
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\omega-h]
,
\beta=dG_{D}[\omega-h]
.

Proof.

First we use Theorem
5.5
. Since we assume that
M
is only
C^{s+1,1}
we get only
G_{D}[\omega-h]\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\alpha,\beta\in W^{\max(s,1),p(\cdot)}(M.\Lambda)
. Since
\alpha,\beta
satisfy the relations (
5.14
) (with
\eta=\omega
), we can use Theorem
7.13
(or Theorem
7.23
for
s=0
) to establish that
\alpha,\beta\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy the required estimate.
∎

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
\omega\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Then there exist
\alpha,\beta\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
h\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
such that

\displaystyle\omega=h+d\alpha+\delta\beta,

\displaystyle n\alpha=0,\quad\delta\alpha=0,\quad n\beta=0,\quad d\beta=0,

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M},\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

These forms are given by
h=\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega
,
\alpha=\delta G_{N}[\omega-h]
,
\beta=dG_{N}[\omega-h]
.

Proof.

We use Theorem
5.11
, and the same argument as in the previous theorem to establish the
W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
regularity for
\alpha
and
\beta
.
∎

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
\omega\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Then there exist
\alpha,\beta\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
h\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap\mathcal{H}(M)
such that

\displaystyle\omega=h+d\alpha+\delta\beta,

\displaystyle t\alpha=0,\quad\delta\alpha=0,\quad n\beta=0,\quad d\beta=0,

\displaystyle\|h\|_{s,p(\cdot),M},\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M},\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

Proof.

Take
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega]
,
\beta=dG_{N}[\omega-\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega]
and let

h=\omega-d\alpha-\delta\beta.

By the same reasoning as in the proof of Theorem
5.17
we get
\alpha,\beta\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and the required estimate.

For any
\xi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
using the integration-by-parts formula (see Lemma
2.8
) and the relation (
5.22
) for
\beta
we find

\displaystyle(h,\delta\xi)=(\omega,\delta\xi)-(\delta\beta,\delta\xi)=(\omega-\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega,\delta\xi)-(\delta\beta,\delta\xi)

\displaystyle=(\delta\beta,\delta\xi)-(\delta\beta,\delta\xi)=0.

Similarly we get
(h,d\xi)=0
for any
\xi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. Thus
dh=0
,
\delta h=0
.
∎

With the principal results on the solvability and regularity of solutions of boundary value problems for the Hodge Laplacian established we proceed to the study of first-order systems of div-curl type.

Chapter 6
Theory of First-Order Systems in Variable Exponent Spaces.

In this Chapter we present results on solvability of (
4.16
), (
4.17
) in variable exponent Sobolev spaces, and related results. Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+1,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
. Recall that by
G_{D}[\eta]
and
G_{N}[\eta]
we denote the Dirichlet and Neumann potentials of
\eta
, respectively, while
\mathcal{P}_{T}
and
\mathcal{P}_{N}
denote the standard projectors on the spaces of Dirichlet and Neumann harmonic fields.

6.1.
Cohomology resolution in variable exponent spaces

In the lemmas below
C
is a quantitative constant which depends on
p_{-}
,
p_{+}
,
c_{\mathrm{log}}(p)
,
M
, and
s
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
df=0
,
tf=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{T}f=0
. Let
G_{D}[f]
be the Dirichlet potential of
f
and
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[f]
. Then

•

f=d\alpha
,
\delta\alpha=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{T}\alpha=0
;

•

\alpha\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.17
we have
f=d\alpha+\delta\beta

where
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[f]
,
\beta=dG_{D}[f]
and

\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

By Corollary
5.6
,
\beta=dG_{D}[f]=G_{D}[df]=G_{D}[0]=0
, thus
f=d\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
df=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{N}f=0
. Let
G_{N}[f]
be the Neumann potential of
f
and
\alpha=\delta G_{N}[f]
. Then

•

f=d\alpha
,
\delta\alpha=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}\alpha=0
;

•

\alpha\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.18
we have
f=d\alpha+\delta\beta

where
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[f]
,
\beta=dG_{D}[f]
and

\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

By Corollary
5.12
,
\beta=dG_{N}[f]=G_{N}[df]=G_{N}[0]=0
, thus
f=d\alpha
.
∎

In the same way we obtain the following results.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
g\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
\delta g=0
,
ng=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{N}g=0
. Let
G_{N}[g]
be the Neumann potential of
g
and
\beta=dG_{N}[g]
. Then

•

g=\delta\beta
,
d\beta=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}\beta=0
;

•

\beta\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|g\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
g\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
\delta g=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{T}g=0
. Let
G_{D}[g]
be the Dirichlet potential of
g
and
\beta=dG_{D}[g]
. Then

•

g=\delta\beta
,
d\beta=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{T}\beta=0
;

•

\beta\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|g\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}
.

6.2.
Gauge fixing

The following statements are immediate corollaries of Lemmas
6.1
,
6.2
,
6.3
,
6.4
respectively.

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
\eta\in W_{T}^{d,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
with
d\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that
d\omega=d\eta
,
\delta\omega=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega=0
, and

(6.1)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|d\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}

with a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
. Precisely, the solution is given by
\omega=\delta G_{D}[d\eta]
.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
\eta\in W^{d,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
with
d\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that
d\omega=d\eta
,
\delta\omega=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega=0
, and

(6.2)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|d\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}

with a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
. Precisely, the solution is given by
\omega=\delta G_{N}[d\eta]
.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
\eta\in W_{N}^{\delta,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
with
\delta\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that
\delta\omega=\delta\eta
,
d\omega=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega=0
, and

(6.3)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\delta\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}

with a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
. Precisely, the solution is given by
\omega=dG_{N}[\delta\eta]
.

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
\eta\in W^{\delta,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
with
d\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then there exists
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that
\delta\omega=\delta\eta
,
d\omega=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega=0
, and

(6.4)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\delta\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}

with a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
. Precisely, the solution is given by
\omega=dG_{T}[\delta\eta]
.

Now we return to the Hodge decomposition. Corollaries
6.5
–
6.8
lead to the following statement.

Corollary 6.9
.

All of the following subspaces are closed in
W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda):

\displaystyle dW^{s+1,p(\cdot)}_{T}(M,\Lambda),\quad dW^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda),\quad dW_{N}^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)

\displaystyle\delta W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}_{N}(M,\Lambda),\quad\delta W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda),\quad\delta W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}_{T}(M,\Lambda).

6.3.
Solvability of first-order systems

In this Section we extend the classical results on solvability of first-order “div-curl” type systems to variable exponent Lebesgue spaces.

Theorem 6.10
.

Let

•

f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
df=0
;

•

v\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\delta v=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{T}v=0
;

•

\varphi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
(f,h_{T})=[\varphi,h_{T}]
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
;

•

and
t(f-d\varphi)=0
.

Then there exists a unique solution
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of the boundary value problem

d\omega=f,\quad\delta\omega=v,\quad t\omega=t\varphi

such that
\mathcal{P}_{T}(\omega-\varphi)=0
and

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|v\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M})

with a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
.
This solution is given by the formula

(6.5)

\omega=\varphi+\delta G_{D}[f-d\varphi]+dG_{D}[v-\delta\varphi].

Proof.

To prove the existence, we introduce
\widetilde{\omega}=\omega-\varphi
, then split it as
\widetilde{\omega}=\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}
and look for solutions to the following two problems:

\displaystyle d\omega_{1}=f-d\varphi,\quad\delta\omega_{1}=0,\quad t\omega_{1}=0,

\displaystyle d\omega_{2}=0,\quad\delta\omega_{2}=v-\delta\varphi,\quad t\omega_{2}=0.

By Lemma
6.1
,

\displaystyle f-d\varphi=d\alpha,\quad\alpha=\delta G_{D}[f-d\varphi]\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda),\quad\delta\alpha=0,

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|f-d\varphi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}).

By Lemma
6.4
,

\displaystyle v-\delta\varphi=\delta\beta,\quad\beta=dG_{D}[v-\delta\varphi]\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda),\quad d\beta=0,

\displaystyle\|\beta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|v-\delta\varphi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}).

It remains to put
\omega_{1}=\alpha
,
\omega_{2}=\beta
. Thus, the required solution has the form (
6.5
).
The uniqueness follows from the fact that a solution of the homogeneous problem is itself in
\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
.
∎

Dual form of this statement for the Neumann boundary conditions has the following form.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let

•

f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
df=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}f=0
;

•

v\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\delta v=0
;

•

\varphi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
(v,h_{N})=-[h_{N},\varphi]
for all
h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
;

•

and
n(g-\delta\varphi)=0
.

Then there exists a unique solution
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of the boundary value problem

d\omega=f,\quad\delta\omega=v,\quad n\omega=n\varphi

such that
\mathcal{P}_{N}(\omega-\varphi)=0
and

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|v\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M})

with a constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
. This solution is given by the formula

(6.6)

\omega=\varphi+\delta G_{N}[f-d\varphi]+dG_{N}[v-\delta\varphi].

Proof.

In this case, using Lemmas
6.2
,
6.3
we construct the required solution in the form (
6.6
).
The uniqueness follows from the fact that a solution of the homogeneous problem is itself in
\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
.
∎

6.4.
Gaffney’s inequality

In this Section we extend the classical Gaffney inequality to variable exponents Sobolev spaces.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\omega\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
have differential
d\omega=f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, satisfy
t\omega=0
, and have codifferential
\delta\omega=v\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then

\displaystyle\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{T}(M,\Lambda),\quad\omega=\mathcal{P_{T}}\omega+\delta G_{D}[f]+dG_{D}[v],

\displaystyle\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|v\|_{s,p(\cdot),M})

where the constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
.

Proof.

The Hodge decomposition of Theorem
5.17
together with Corollary
5.6
give

\omega=\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega+d\delta G_{D}[\omega]+\delta dG_{D}[\omega]=\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega+dG_{D}[f]+\delta G_{D}[v].

The differentiability properties of
\omega
now follow from Theorem
5.5
.
∎

The dual form of this statement is

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
\omega\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
have differential
d\omega=f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, codifferential
\delta\omega=v\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, and satisfy
n\omega=0
. Then

\displaystyle\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)\cap W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{N}(M),\quad\omega=\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega+\delta G_{N}[f]+dG_{D}[v],

\displaystyle\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|v\|_{s,p(\cdot),M})

where the constant
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
.

An alternative proof of the Gaffney inequality which does not require solving auxiliary boundary value problems will be presented in Section
7.6
.

6.5.
Cohomological minimizers in variable exponent spaces

As a consequence, we can now prove the following result, which is instrumental for dealing with quasilinear problems in variable exponent spaces. With our Gaffney inequality in Section
6.4
(or Section
7.6
), this can now be proved analogously to the proof of Theorem 6.4 in
[
Sil19a
]
or Theorem 5.1 in
[
BDS15
]
.

In the following statement we work in a domain
\Omega
in the Euclidian space, so we do not make distinction between even and odd forms. Let
W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\delta,T}=\{u\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{T}(\Omega,\Lambda)\,:\,\delta u=0\}
. The condition
\int\limits_{\Omega}u=\int\limits_{\Omega}u_{0}
in the statement below relates only to the top degree components, since the integrals of other components over
\Omega
vanish by definition. Note that while the integral functional below does not depend on the top degree component of the form
u
, this component will be exactly the top degree component of the form
u_{0}
.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
C^{1,1}
bounded, contractible domain. Let
a:\Omega\rightarrow[\gamma,L]
be measurable, where
0<\gamma<L<\infty.
Let
p(\cdot)
satisfy (
1.1
), (
1.2
);
F\in L^{p^{\prime}(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)
,
p^{\prime}(x)=\frac{p(x)}{p(x)-1}
,
u_{0}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)
, and

I[u]:=\int\limits_{\Omega}\left[\frac{a\left(x\right)}{{p\left(x\right)}}\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)}-\left\langle F,du\right\rangle\right]\,dx\qquad\text{ for }u\in u_{0}+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\delta,T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right).

Then the following minimization problem

\displaystyle\inf\left\{I[u]\,:\,u\in u_{0}+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\delta,T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right),\ \int\limits_{\Omega}u=\int\limits_{\Omega}u_{0}\right\}

admits a unique minimizer. Moreover, the minimizer
\bar{u}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)
is the unique weak solution to the system

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \delta\left(a\left(x\right)\left\lvert d\bar{u}\right\rvert^{p(x)-2}d\bar{u}\right)&=\delta F&&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\delta\bar{u}&=\delta u_{0}&&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
t\bar{u}&=tu_{0}&&\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, using Young’s inequality and integrating, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\langle F,du\right\rangle\,dx\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\lvert F\right\rvert\left\lvert du\right\rvert\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{\Omega}\frac{\varepsilon^{p(x)}}{p(x)}\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)}\,dx+\int\limits_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p^{\prime}(x)}p^{\prime}(x)}\left\lvert F\right\rvert^{p^{\prime}(x)}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq{\varepsilon^{p_{-}}\int\limits_{\Omega}\frac{1}{p(x)}\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)}\,dx}+C_{p,\varepsilon}\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\lvert F\right\rvert^{p^{\prime}(x)}\,dx,

where
C_{p,\varepsilon}>0
depends only on
\varepsilon,p_{-}
and
p_{+}.
Thus, by choosing
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
sufficiently small, we deduce

\displaystyle I[u]

\displaystyle\geq\gamma{\int\limits_{\Omega}\frac{1}{p(x)}\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)}\,dx}-\left\lvert\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\langle F,du\right\rangle\,dx\right\rvert

\displaystyle\geq{\left(\gamma-\varepsilon^{p_{-}}\right)\int\limits_{\Omega}\frac{1}{p(x)}\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)}\,dx}-C_{p,\varepsilon}\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\lvert F\right\rvert^{p^{\prime}(x)}\leq-C\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\lvert F\right\rvert^{p^{\prime}(x)}.

This shows
I[u]
is bounded below. The same estimate shows that we can choose
\varepsilon>0
small enough to have the bound

\displaystyle I[u]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\gamma}{2}{\int\limits_{\Omega}\frac{1}{p(x)}\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)}\,dx}\geq{\frac{\gamma}{2p_{-}}\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)}\,dx}.

Thus, if
\left\{u_{s}\right\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}\subset u_{0}+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\delta,T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)
is a minimizing sequence for
I,
then
\left\lVert du_{s}\right\rVert_{L^{p(\cdot)}}
is uniformly bounded and
\delta u_{s}=\delta u_{0}
for every
s\in\mathbb{N}.
Since
\Omega
is contractible,

\mathcal{H}_{T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda^{r}\right)=\left\{0\right\},\quad r<n,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{H}_{T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda^{n}\right)=\left\{cdx^{1}\ldots dx^{n},\,c\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

So
\mathcal{P}_{T}(u_{s}-u_{0})=0
and applying the variable exponent Gaffney inequality of Lemma
6.12
to
u_{s}-u_{0,}
we deduce

\displaystyle\left\lVert u_{s}\right\rVert_{W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert u_{s}-u_{0}\right\rVert_{W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)}+\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert du_{s}-du_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)}+\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert du_{s}\right\rVert_{L^{p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)}+c\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)}.

Thus,
\left\{u_{s}\right\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right).
As
W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\delta,T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)
is a linear subspace and consequently a convex subset of
W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right),
this implies that up to the extraction of a subsequence that we do not relabel, there exists
u\in u_{0}+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\delta,T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)
such that

u_{s}\rightharpoonup u\qquad\text{ weakly in }W^{1,p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right).

This implies

du_{s}\rightharpoonup du\qquad\text{ weakly in }L^{p(\cdot)}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right).

Now set
\phi\left(x,t\right)=a(x)\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{p(x)}.
Using Theorem 2.2.8 in
[
DHHR11
]
, we have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega}a\left(x\right)\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)}\,dx\leq\liminf\limits_{s\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\Omega}a\left(x\right)\left\lvert du_{s}\right\rvert^{p(x)}\,dx.

Since the weak convergence in
L^{p(\cdot)}
also implies

\int_{\Omega}\left\langle F,du_{s}\right\rangle\,dx\rightarrow\int_{\Omega}\left\langle F,du\right\rangle\,dx,

standard techniques in the direct methods in the calculus of variations implies
u\in u_{0}+W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\delta,T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right)
is a minimizer, which also satisfies the weak form of the Euler-Lagrange equations

(6.7)

\begin{gathered}\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\langle a(x)\left\lvert du\right\rvert^{p(x)-2}du-F,d\phi\right\rangle=0\qquad\text{ for all }\phi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\delta,T}\left(\Omega,\Lambda\right).\end{gathered}

By the gauge fixing result of Corollary
6.5
, the Euler-Lagrange equation (
6.7
) holds also for all
\varphi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{T}(\Omega,\Lambda)
. Uniqueness follows from the fact that the function
t\mapsto\phi\left(x,t\right)
is strictly convex for a.e.
x\in\Omega
.
∎

6.6.
Natural boundary conditions for the Hodge Laplacian

In this Section we assume that
M
is of the class
C^{s+1,1}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. For Theorem
6.20
on solvability of the boundary value problem with natural boundary conditions (that is, setting the tangential part of the codifferential and the normal part of the differential) we shall assume
M
to be
C^{s+2,1}
. The case of
C^{1,1}
manifolds will be considered separately in Theorem
6.21
. As is well known, this problem is non-elliptic, with infinite dimensional (co)kernel so its solution is usually constructed in a roundabout way using already constructed solutions to problems with Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions.

For
\omega\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
recall the decomposition
\omega=h+d\alpha+\delta\beta
from Theorem
5.19
and denote

\mathcal{P}\omega=h=\omega-d\alpha-\delta\beta=\omega-d\delta G_{D}[\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega]-\delta dG_{N}[\omega-\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega].

By construction,
\mathcal{P}\omega
is a bounded operator on
W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
:

\|\mathcal{P}\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)\|\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M},

and
d\mathcal{P}\omega=0
,
\delta\mathcal{P}\omega=0
. By the Gaffney inequality, clearly
\mathcal{P}\omega\in W^{s+1,q}_{loc}(M,\Lambda)
for any
q<\infty
.

If
\omega\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
then
\omega=d\alpha+d\beta
with
\alpha\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
and
\beta\in W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and so
(\omega,h)=0
for any
h\in\mathcal{H}(M,\Lambda)\cap L^{q}(M,\Lambda)
with
q\geq p_{-}^{\prime}=p_{-}/(p_{-}-1)
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
implies that
\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega=0
and
\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega=0
, so

\omega=d\alpha+\delta\beta,\quad\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\omega],\quad\beta=dG_{N}[\omega].

From Corollaries
5.6
and
5.12
it follows that for
\omega\in\mathcal{H}(M)\cap L^{q}(M,\Lambda)
,
q>1
, there holds
\mathcal{P}\omega=\omega
. For
\omega\in L^{q}(M,\Lambda)
,
q\geq 2
, the projector
\mathcal{P}
coincides with the usual orthogonal projector on the space
\mathcal{H}(M)\cap L^{2}(M,\Lambda)
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{H}(M)\cap L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
(\omega,h)=0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}(M)\cap(\cap_{q>1}W^{s,q}(M,\Lambda))
. Then
\omega=0
.

Proof.

Let
\eta\in W^{s,q}(M,\Lambda)
. Then in the Hodge decomposition of Theorem
5.19
we get
\eta=h+d\alpha+\delta\beta
, where
h\in W^{s,q}(\Omega)
,
\alpha,\beta\in W^{s+1,q}(\Omega)
for any
q<\infty
,
t\alpha=0
and
n\beta=0
. Thus
(\omega,d\alpha)=0
,
(\omega,\delta\beta)=0
, and so

(\omega,\eta)=(\omega,h)=0.

Since
\eta
is arbitrary,
\omega=0
.
∎

Corollary 6.16
.

If
(\omega,h)=0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}(M)\cap(\cap_{q>1}W^{s,q}(M,\Lambda))
then
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
.

Proof.

Follows from the fact that

(\mathcal{P}\omega,h)=(\omega-d\alpha-\delta\beta,h)=(\omega,h)=0

for such
h
. By the previous Lemma,
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
.
∎

Corollary 6.17
.

If
\omega=dA+\delta B
,
A,B\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
tA=0
,
nB=0
, then
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.16
it is sufficient to check that
(\omega,h)=0
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}(M)\cap(\cap_{q>1}W^{s,q}(M,\Lambda))
. But this follows from the assumptions of the Corollary and approximation results:
(dA+\delta B,h)=0
.
∎

If one assumes more regularity on
M
, say
M
is of the class
C^{k+2,1}
(or
C^{k+3,\alpha}
), then one can test
\omega
only against harmonic fields from
\cap_{q>1}W^{k,q}(M,\Lambda)
(resp.
C^{k,\alpha}(M,\Lambda)
).

Also directly from Theorem
5.19
and Corollaries
5.6
,
5.12
we obtain a result similar to Lemmas
6.12
,
6.13
.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
\omega\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
have differential
d\omega=f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and codifferential
\delta\omega=v\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega-\mathcal{P}\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,

\omega-\mathcal{P}\omega=dG_{D}[v]+\delta G_{N}[f],

and

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|v\|_{s,p(\cdot),M})

where
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
.

Theorem 6.19
.

(i) Let
M\in C^{1,1}
and and
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
with
\mathcal{P}\eta=0
. Then there exists
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
and
d\omega,\delta\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
which solves the boundary value problem

(6.8)

\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\delta\omega=0,\quad nd\omega=0,

and satisfies

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|d\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}.

(ii) Let
M\in C^{s+2,1}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be such that
\mathcal{P}\eta=0
. Then there exists a solution
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of the boundary value problem (
6.8
) such that
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
and

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}

where
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.19
and the definition of the projector
\mathcal{P}
we have
\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta=0
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}\eta=0
and the Hodge decomposition

\eta=d\alpha+\delta\beta,\quad\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\eta],\quad\beta=dG_{N}[\eta].

We look for a solution to the problem

d\omega=\beta=dG_{N}[\eta],\quad\delta\omega=\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\eta]

in the form

\omega=G_{D}[\eta]+G_{N}[\eta]+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}

where

\displaystyle d\mu_{1}=-dG_{D}[\eta],\quad\delta\mu_{1}=0,\quad t\mu_{1}=0,

\displaystyle d\mu_{2}=0,\quad\delta\mu_{2}=-\delta G_{N}[\eta],\quad n\mu_{2}=0.

By Corollaries
6.5
,
6.7
, we can construct
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
in the form

\mu_{1}=\delta G_{D}[-dG_{D}[\eta]],\quad\mu_{2}=dG_{N}[-\delta G_{N}[\eta]].

By Corollaries
5.6
,
5.12
,

\mu_{1}=-G_{D}[\delta dG_{D}[\eta]],\quad\mu_{2}=-G_{N}[d\delta G_{N}[\eta]].

Thus

\displaystyle\omega=G_{D}[\eta-\delta dG_{D}[\eta]]+G_{N}[\eta-d\delta G_{N}[\eta]]

\displaystyle=G_{D}[d\delta G_{D}[\eta]]+G_{N}[\delta dG_{N}[\eta]]

\displaystyle=dG_{D}[\delta G_{D}[\eta]]+\delta G_{N}[dG_{N}[\eta]].

It clearly satisfies (
6.8
),
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
, and provides a solution with the required estimates.
∎

Now we shall deal with the non-homogeneous“natural” boundary value problem for the Hodge Laplacian assuming that
M
is of the class
C^{s+2,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 6.20
.

Let
M\in C^{s+2,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
. Let
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be such that
\mathcal{P}(\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi)=0
. Then there exists a solution
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of the boundary value problem

(6.9)

\triangle\omega=\eta,\quad t\delta\omega=t\varphi,\quad nd\omega=n\psi,

such that

\|\omega\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M})

where
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
.

Proof.

First, by Corollary
3.18
we construct
\gamma\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that

\displaystyle t\gamma=0,\quad n\gamma=0,\quad t\delta\gamma=t\varphi,\quad nd\gamma=n\psi,

\displaystyle\|\gamma\|_{s+2,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).

Consider the boundary value problem

\triangle\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\eta}=\eta-d\alpha-\delta\beta,\quad t\delta\widetilde{\omega}=0,\quad nd\widetilde{\omega}=0,

where

\alpha=\delta\gamma,\quad\beta=d\gamma,\quad t\alpha=t\varphi,\quad n\beta=n\psi.

By Corollary
6.17
there holds

\mathcal{P}(\widetilde{\eta})=\mathcal{P}(\eta-d\alpha-\delta\beta)=\mathcal{P}(\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi)+\mathcal{P}(d(\varphi-\alpha)+\delta(\psi-\beta))=0.

It remains to use Theorem
6.19
and then set
\omega=\widetilde{\omega}+\gamma-\mathcal{P}[\widetilde{\omega}+\gamma]
.
∎

On
C^{1,1}
manifolds for
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
we can understand a solution of the boundary value problem (
6.9
) in the weak sense, namely

(6.10)

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)=(\eta,\zeta)+[\zeta,\psi]-[\varphi,\zeta]

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}\,(M,\Lambda)
. On
C^{2,1}
manifolds, assuming that
\omega\in W^{2,p_{-}}
, this definition yields (
6.9
) upon integration-by-parts.

Assuming that
\eta\in L^{2}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
, and
\mathcal{P}(\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi)=0
a solution to (
6.10
) is easily constructed. Consider first the variational problem

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega)-(\eta,\omega)-[\omega,\psi]+[\varphi,\omega]\rightarrow\min

over
\omega\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
. A unique minimizer with
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
exists by the direct method of the calculus of variations and the Gaffney inequality of Lemma
6.18
(see Section
4.2
). This minimizer satisfies (
6.10
) as the Euler-Lagrange equation.

Now let
M
be still only
C^{1,1}
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Consider the Lipschitz approximations
\eta_{\varepsilon}\to\eta
in
L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\to\varphi
,
\psi_{\varepsilon}\to\psi
in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Let

p_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{P}(\eta_{\varepsilon}-\delta\psi_{\varepsilon}-d\varphi_{\varepsilon}).

The sequence
p_{\varepsilon}\to 0
in
L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Let
\omega_{\varepsilon}
be the unique minimizer of the corresponding variational problem

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega)-(\eta_{\varepsilon}-p_{\varepsilon},\omega)-[\zeta,\psi_{\varepsilon}]+[\varphi_{\varepsilon},\zeta]\to\min

over
\omega\in W^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
with
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
. Then
\omega_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the variational relation

\mathcal{D}(\omega_{\varepsilon},\zeta)=(\eta_{\varepsilon}-\delta\psi_{\varepsilon}-d\varphi_{\varepsilon}-p_{\varepsilon},\zeta)+(d\zeta,\psi_{\varepsilon})+(\delta\zeta,\varphi_{\varepsilon})

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}\,(M,\Lambda)
. By Theorem
7.28
there holds
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=\delta\omega_{\varepsilon}
and
\beta_{\varepsilon}=d\omega_{\varepsilon}
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy the variational relations

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\alpha_{\varepsilon},\zeta)=(\eta_{\varepsilon}-\delta\psi_{\varepsilon}-p_{\varepsilon},d\zeta),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\beta_{\varepsilon},\zeta)=(\eta_{\varepsilon}-d\varphi_{\varepsilon}-p_{\varepsilon},\delta\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}\,(M,\Lambda)
together with the boundary conditions
t\alpha_{\varepsilon}=t\varphi_{\varepsilon}
,
n\beta_{\varepsilon}=n\psi_{\varepsilon}
. By Theorem
7.23
and Lemmas
5.2
,
5.8
, there holds

\displaystyle\|\alpha_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq\|\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta_{\varepsilon}-\delta\psi_{\varepsilon}-d\varphi_{\varepsilon}-p_{\varepsilon}\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{p(\cdot),M}),

\displaystyle\|\beta_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq\|\psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta_{\varepsilon}-\delta\psi_{\varepsilon}-d\varphi_{\varepsilon}-p_{\varepsilon}\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|d\psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{p(\cdot),M}).

By Lemma
6.18
we obtain the estimate

\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta_{\varepsilon}-\delta\psi_{\varepsilon}-d\varphi_{\varepsilon}-p_{\varepsilon}\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1,p(\cdot),M})

which provides the uniform bound on
\omega_{\varepsilon}
in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. By the same estimates applied to the difference of
\omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\omega_{\varepsilon_{2}}
we conclude that the sequences
\alpha_{\varepsilon},\beta_{\varepsilon},\omega_{\varepsilon}
are fundamental and therefore converge in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. The limit form
\omega=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\omega_{\varepsilon}
possesses the differential
d\omega=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\beta_{\varepsilon}
and codifferential
\delta\omega_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\alpha_{\varepsilon}
, which belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. We formulate this result as

Theorem 6.21
.

Let
M\in C^{1,1}
. Let
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be such that
\mathcal{P}(\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi)=0
. Then there exists a (weak) solution
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
of the boundary value problem (
6.9
) such that
d\omega,\delta\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
, and

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|d\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}).

We can also construct a solution to the boundary value problem (
6.9
) in an explicit form using solutions of auxiliary Dirichlet and Neumann problems, similar to the construction used in the proof of Theorem
6.19
. Consider the boundary value problems

\displaystyle\triangle A=\eta-\delta\psi\quad\text{in}\quad M,\quad tA=0\quad\text{and}\quad t\delta A=t\varphi\quad\text{on}\quad bM,

\displaystyle\triangle B=\eta-d\varphi\quad\text{in}\quad M,\quad nB=0\quad\text{and}\quad ndB=n\psi\quad\text{on}\quad bM.

Let

\omega=dG_{D}[\delta A]+\delta G_{N}[dB].

Then using the commutation relations of Corollaries
5.6
,
5.12
we get

\displaystyle d\omega=d\delta G_{N}[dB]=dB-\delta dG_{N}[dB]=dB,

\displaystyle\delta\omega=\delta dG_{D}[\delta A]=\delta A-d\delta G_{D}[\delta A]=\delta A.

Thus
\omega
satisfies the required boundary conditions
t\delta\omega=t\varphi
and
nd\omega=n\psi
, and
\mathcal{P}\omega=0
. It remains to check that
\triangle\omega=\eta
. The potentials
dA
,
\delta A
,
dB
,
\delta B
satisfy the variational relations

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\delta A,\zeta)=(\eta-\delta\psi,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(dA,\zeta)=(\eta-\delta\psi-d\varphi,\delta\zeta),\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\delta B,\zeta)=(\eta-\delta\psi-d\varphi,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(dB,\zeta)=(\eta-d\varphi,\delta\zeta),\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda).

In particular,
dA=dG_{D}[\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi]
,
\delta B=\delta G_{N}[\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi]
.

The condition
\mathcal{P}(\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi)=0
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi=du+\delta v,\quad u\in W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda),\quad v\in W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda),

\displaystyle u=\delta G_{D}[\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi],\quad v=dG_{N}[\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi].

Finally we calculate

\triangle\omega=d\delta A+\delta dB=\eta+(\eta-d\varphi-\delta\psi)-\delta dA-d\delta B=\eta.

This argument provides an alternative proof of Theorems
6.20
,
6.21
. The proof of Theorem
6.21
via the variational argument is given to stress the fact that the problem, while being non-elliptic, can still be understood in the variational setting.

6.7.
Hodge-Dirac system

In a standard way, solvability of the boundary value problems for the Hodge Laplacian gives solvability of boundary value problems for the Hodge-Dirac operator.

Let
D=d+\alpha\delta
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. The important cases are (symmetric)
d+\delta
and (skew-symmetric)
d-\delta
(on forms with vanishing tangential/normal part on
bM
). Let
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 6.22
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be such that
\mathcal{P}_{T}f=0
. Then there exists
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfying
D\omega=F
a.e. in
M
and
t\omega=0
, such that
(\omega,\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))=0
and

(6.11)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s,\alpha)\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

Proof.

Take
\omega=DG_{D}[\alpha^{-1}f]
, that is
f
defined by (
2.1
) is mapped to

\omega=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\omega_{e}^{r}+\sum_{r=0}^{n}\omega_{o}^{r},

where
\omega_{*}^{(0)}=\delta G_{D}[f_{*}^{1}]
,
\omega_{*}^{j}=\alpha^{-1}dG_{D}[f_{*}^{j-1}]+\delta G_{D}[f_{*}^{j+1}]
,
j=1,\ldots,n-1
and
\omega_{*}^{n}=\alpha^{-1}dG_{D}[f_{*}^{n-1}]
,
*\in\{e,o\}
.
∎

Theorem 6.23
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be such that
\mathcal{P}_{N}f=0
. Then there exists
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfying
D\omega=F
a.e. in
M
and
n\omega=0
, such that
(\omega,\mathcal{H}_{N}(M))=0
and (
6.11
) holds.

Proof.

Take
\omega=DG_{N}[\alpha^{-1}f]
.
∎

The following a priori estimate is valid.

Corollary 6.24
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and let
\omega\in W_{T}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
D\omega=f
, a.e. in
M
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s,\alpha)\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

Proof.

First note that
D\omega=f
and
t\omega=0
imply
(f,h_{T})=0
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
. Let
\eta\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M)
,
t\omega=0
, be a solution to
D\omega=\eta
guaranteed by Theorem
6.22
, then

\|\eta\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s,\alpha)\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}

Then
D(\omega-\eta)=0
. Let
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. Then from the integration-by-parts formula (for
C^{1,1}
manifolds see Lemma
2.8
) it follows that

0=(D(\omega-\eta),d\zeta+\alpha\delta^{-1}\zeta)=(d(\omega-\zeta)+\alpha\delta(\omega-\zeta),d\zeta+\alpha\delta^{-1}\zeta)=\mathcal{D}(\omega-\eta,\zeta).

From Theorems
7.13
,
7.23
it follows that
\omega-\eta\in W^{s+1,q}(M,\Lambda)
for any
q<\infty
, and also
\omega-\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. By approximation one can take the test form
\zeta=\omega-\eta
, so
\mathcal{D}(\omega-\eta,\omega-\eta)=0
, which implies
\omega-\eta\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
. This proves the claim.
∎

Corollary 6.25
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and let
\omega\in W_{N}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
D\omega=f
, a.e. in
M
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{N}\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s,\alpha)\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

Corollary 6.26
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and let
\omega\in W_{0}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy
D\omega=f
, a.e. in
M
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s,\alpha)\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}.

We also state a variant of Gaffney’s inequality.

Theorem 6.27
.

Let
M
be
C^{1,1}
. Let
\omega\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
and
f\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy

(6.12)

(\omega,\alpha d\zeta+\delta\zeta)=(f,\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
and
D\omega=f
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\omega}\in W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
be a solution to
D\widetilde{\omega}=f
guaranteed by Theorem
6.22
. Then
\hat{\omega}=\omega-\widetilde{\omega}
satisfies

(\hat{\omega},\alpha d\zeta+\delta\zeta)=0\quad\text{for all}\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda).

Let
g\in L^{p_{-}^{\prime}}(M,\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{P}_{T}g=0
. Then by Theorem
6.22
(with
\alpha
replaced by its reciprocal) there exists
\zeta\in W_{T}^{1,p_{-}^{\prime}}(M,\Lambda)
such that
\alpha d\zeta_{g}+\delta\zeta_{g}=g
. By approximation, we have

(\hat{\omega},g)=(\hat{\omega},\alpha d\zeta_{g}+\delta\zeta_{g})=0.

Then

(\hat{\omega}-\mathcal{P}_{T}\hat{\omega},g)=0\quad\text{for all}\quad g\in L^{p_{-}^{\prime}}(M,\Lambda).

Thus
\hat{\omega}-\mathcal{P}_{T}\hat{\omega}=0
and so
\omega=\widetilde{\omega}+h_{T}
,
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
. Once we know that
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
we can use integration-by-parts in (
6.12
) to get

(d\omega+\alpha\delta\omega,\zeta)=(f,\zeta)+[\omega,\zeta]\quad\text{for all}\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda).

This immediately implies
D\omega=f
and
t\omega=0
and completes the proof of Theorem
6.27
.
∎

The proof of the following theorem is similar or may be obtained by the Hodge duality.

Theorem 6.28
.

Let
M
be
C^{1,1}
. Let
\omega\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
and
f\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
6.12
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p_{\cdot}}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
and
D\omega=f
.

Remark 6.29
.

It is also possible to prove Theorems
6.27
,
6.28
in vein of local a priori estimates similar to Lemma
7.19
. See the end of Section
7.6
.

6.8.
Non-elliptic first-order systems

In this Section we extend to the variable exponent setting some well-known results on the boundary value problems for the operators
d
and
\delta
.

Theorem 6.30
.

Let
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, be such that

df=0,\quad t(f-d\varphi)=0,\quad(f,h_{T})=[\varphi,h_{T}]

for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}(M)
. Then the problem

(6.13)

d\omega=f,\quad\omega=\varphi\quad\text{on}\quad bM.

has a solution in
W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that

(6.14)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).

Proof.

By Lemma
6.1
we get

\displaystyle f-d\varphi=d\delta G_{D}[f-d\varphi],

\displaystyle\|\delta G_{D}[f-d\varphi]\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).

By Corollary
3.18
, there exists
\gamma\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that
d\gamma\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

t\gamma=0,\quad n\gamma=0,\quad nd\gamma=n\delta G_{D}[f-d\varphi],

and

\|d\gamma\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C\|\delta G_{D}[f-d\varphi]\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}.

Then

\omega=\delta G_{D}[f-d\varphi]-d\gamma+\varphi

solves (
6.13
) and satisfies (
6.14
).
∎

The dual form of this statement is

Theorem 6.31
.

Let
v\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, be such that

\delta v=0,\quad n(g-\delta\psi)=0,\quad(g,h_{N})=-[h_{N},\psi]

for all
h_{N}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}(M)
. Then the problem

(6.15)

\delta\omega=v,\quad\omega=\psi\quad\text{on}\quad bM.

has a solution in
W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
such that

(6.16)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|v\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).

Chapter 7
A Priori Estimates in Variable Exponent Spaces

This Chapter contains a general regularity result, Lemmas
5.1
,
5.7
,
5.13
above are its special cases. First, in Section
7.1
we write variational relation (
4.5
) in coordinates. In Section
7.2
we present construction of potentials corresponding to the boundary value problems for the Hodge Laplacian. In Section
7.3
we pass to the localized version of (
4.5
) and represent its solution via potentials from Section
7.2
. In Sections
7.4
,
7.5
this representation is used to derive elliptic estimates in variable exponent spaces. Section
7.6
we obtain Gaffney’s inequality under natural regularity assumptions on the manifold. In Section
7.7
we complete the proof of
W^{1,p(\cdot)}
estimates on
C^{1,1}
manifolds. In the last Section
7.8
we prove additional regularity for potentials on
C^{1,1}
manifolds following the line of Section
7.6
.

By
B_{r}
we denote an open ball of radius
r
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
B_{r}(x)
is the open ball of radius
r
centered at
x
,
B_{r}^{+}(x)=B_{r}(x)\cap\{x^{n}>0\}
,
\overline{B}_{r}^{+}(x)=B_{r}(x)\cap\{x^{n}\geq 0\}
. Without loss it is sufficient to consider only the case when
\omega
is a homogeneous form (the only exception is the Hodge-Dirac system in Remark
7.21
).

7.1.
Variational formulation in coordinates

Below in boundary coordinate patches we shall use the admissible coordinate systems
[
Mor66
]
[Lemma 7.5.1]. Let
(U_{\alpha},h_{\alpha})
be a finite coordinate atlas such that if
U_{\alpha}
intersects the boundary, the coordinate system
h_{\alpha}
is admissible. Let
\varepsilon>0
. We shall use a finite refinement
(V_{\beta},\widetilde{h}_{\beta})
of the coordinate atlas
(U_{\alpha},h_{\alpha})
such that

•

If
V_{\beta}
intersects the boundary the coordinate system
\widetilde{h}_{\beta}
is admissible,

•

for all
\beta
there exists
\alpha
and an affine transformation
T_{\alpha\beta}
satisfying
V_{\beta}\subset U_{\alpha}
,
T_{\alpha\beta}\widetilde{h}_{\beta}=h_{\alpha}
on
V_{\beta}
, and the norms of differentials of such transformations and their inverses are uniformly bounded (
\|DT_{\alpha\beta}\|^{2}\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}
,
\|DT_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}\|^{2}\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}
, where
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}
,
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}
are the maximal and minimal eigenvalue of the matrix
g_{ij}
in coordinate system
h_{\alpha}
).

•

\widetilde{h}_{\beta}(V_{\beta})=B_{\varepsilon}(0)
for interior patches or half-ball
B_{\varepsilon}^{+}(0)
for boundary patches and
T_{\alpha\beta}B_{2\varepsilon}(0)\subset h_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})
(resp.
T_{\alpha\beta}\overline{B}^{+}_{2\varepsilon}(0)\subset h_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})
).

•

and in any coordinate system
\widetilde{h}_{\beta}
we have
g_{ij}(0)=\delta_{ij}
.

Such atlas exists for all
\varepsilon>0
and will be further referred to as
\varepsilon
-atlas. In particular, this guarantees that in all the coordinate charts of this atlas the coefficients (and their derivatives) will have the uniform bounds independent on
\varepsilon
.

By the Lebesgue lemma, for a sufficiently small
r_{*}>0
each open ball of radius
r_{*}
in
M
belongs to one of
U_{\alpha}
. Thus for a given
c\geq 1
there exists
r_{0}>0
such that for any
r\in(0,r_{0})
there exists a finite cover of manifold by balls
\mathcal{B}_{r}(P_{j})
,
j=1,\ldots,N
, of radius
r
such that each concentric ball
\mathcal{B}_{cr}(P_{j})
of radius
cr
belongs to one of
U_{\alpha}
and either has an empty intersection with
bM
or
P_{j}\in bM
. It immediately follows that for each
c\geq 1
there exists
r_{1}>0
such that for each
r\in(0,r_{1})
there exists a finite cover of
M
by the open sets of the form
\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}(B_{r}(P_{j,\alpha}))
where
B_{cr}(P_{j,\alpha})\subset\varphi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})
and in boundary patches either
B_{4r}(P_{j,\alpha})\subset\{x^{n}>0\}
or
P_{j,\alpha}\in\{x^{n}=0\}
. Let
\widetilde{B}_{r}(P_{j,\alpha})
be a ball in
\varphi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})
centered at
P_{j,\alpha}
with respect to the metric defined by the metric tensor in its center. There exists
r_{2}>0
such that for each
r\in(0,r_{2})
there exists a finite open cover of
M
by the sets of the form
\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\widetilde{B}_{r}(P_{j,\alpha}))
where
\widetilde{B}_{cr}(P_{j,\alpha})\subset\varphi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})
and in boundary patches either
\widetilde{B}_{4r}(P_{j,\alpha})\subset\{x^{n}>0\}
or
P_{j,\alpha}\in\{x^{n}=0\}
.

We shall work with forms satisfying the variational equation (
4.5
), that is

(d\omega-\varphi,d\zeta)+(\delta\omega-\psi,\delta\zeta)-(\eta,\zeta)=0

for a suitable class of test forms
\zeta
.

Let
\omega
be such that one of the following holds

(1)

(
problem with full Dirichlet data
)
\omega\in W_{0}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
and the variational equation (
4.5
) holds for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
;

(2)

(
Dirichlet problem
)
\omega\in W_{T}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
and the variational equation (
4.5
) holds for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
;

(3)

(
Neumann problem
)
\omega\in W_{N}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
and the variational equation (
4.5
) holds for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
.

Further we can assume that
\omega
is a homogeneous form of degree
r
.

Let
\mathcal{I}(r)
be the set of ordered multi-indices
I=1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\ldots<i_{r}\leq n
and let
\mathcal{I}_{0}
denote a subset of
\mathcal{I}(r)
such that in boundary coordinate patches for the Dirichlet problem
\mathcal{I}_{0}
consists of
I\in\mathcal{I}(r)
with
i_{r}<n
, and for the Neumann problem
\mathcal{I}_{0}\in\mathcal{I}(r)
consists of
I
with
i_{r}=n
. The case when
\mathcal{I}_{0}=\mathcal{I}(r)
corresponds to the boundary conditions
t\omega=0
,
n\omega=0
. For interior coordinate patches we set
\mathcal{I}_{0}=\emptyset
.

Let
G_{2R}=\widetilde{G}_{2R}=B_{2R}
for an interior coordinate patch, or
G_{2R}=B_{2R}^{+}=B_{2R}\cap\{x_{n}>0\}
and
\widetilde{G}_{2R}=\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
for a boundary coordinate patch. Recall that by
\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
we denote
B_{2R}\cap\{x^{n}\geq 0\}
.

By
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
we shall denote the set of functions
\{\zeta_{I}\}
,
I\in\mathcal{I}(r)
, from
C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{B}_{2R}^{+})
such that
\zeta_{I}=0
near
B_{2R}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
. For a (variable) exponent
q(\cdot)
by
W^{1,q(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
, we denote the set of functions
\{\omega_{I}\}
,
I\in\mathcal{I}(r)
, such that
\omega_{I}=0
on
B_{2R}\cap\{x_{n}=0\}
for all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
(in the sense of trace). For
s\in\mathbb{N}
by
W^{s,p(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
we mean
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})\cap W^{s,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
.

Then
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
and equation (
4.5
), that is

(d\omega-\varphi,d\zeta)+(\delta\omega-\psi,\delta\zeta)-(\eta,\zeta)=0,

takes the form:

(7.1)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{G_{2R}}\zeta_{I,\alpha}

\displaystyle[a^{IJ\alpha\beta}\omega_{J,\beta}+b^{IJ\alpha}\omega_{J}+e^{I\alpha}]\,dx

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{G_{2R}}\zeta_{I}[b^{*IJ\beta}\omega_{J,\beta}+c^{IJ}\omega_{J}+f^{I}]\,dx=0

for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
.

The coefficients

•

a^{IJ\alpha\beta}=\widetilde{a}^{IJ\alpha\beta}\sqrt{g}
, where
\widetilde{a}^{IJ\alpha\beta}
is a tensor field on
M
involving only the metric: for co-vector fields
z
,
\widetilde{z}
there holds

\displaystyle a^{IJ\alpha\beta}\omega_{J}\xi_{I}z_{\alpha}\widetilde{z}_{\beta}=\langle\widetilde{z}\wedge\omega,z\wedge\zeta\rangle+\langle\widetilde{z}\lrcorner\omega,z\lrcorner\zeta\rangle,

\displaystyle a^{IJ\alpha\beta}=a^{JI\beta\alpha},\quad a^{IJ\alpha\beta}+a^{IJ\beta\alpha}=2G^{IJ}g^{\alpha\beta}

•

b^{IJ\alpha}
,
b^{*IJ\alpha}
,
c^{IJ}
involve the Christoffel symbols and the metric,
b^{*IJ\alpha}=b^{JI\alpha}
and
c^{IJ}=c^{JI}
;

•

e^{I\alpha}
are linear combinations of the components of
\varphi
and
\psi
with coefficients depending only on the metric.

•

f^{I}
are linear combinations of the components of
\psi
and
\eta
;

•

of
\eta
in
f
involve only the metric coefficients;

•

of
\psi
in
f
involve also the derivatives of metric via Christoffel symbols.

Thus, for a manifold of the class
C^{s+2,1}
,
s\geq-1
, we have

•

a^{IJ\alpha\beta}\in C^{s+1,1}(G_{2R})
;

•

b^{IJ\alpha},b^{*IJ\alpha},c^{IJ}\in C^{s,1}(G_{2R})
if
s\geq 0
and
b^{IJ\alpha},b^{*IJ\alpha},c^{IJ}\in L^{\infty}(G_{2R})
if
s=-1
;

•

f^{I}=C_{1}^{IJ}\eta_{J}+C_{2}^{IJ}\psi_{J}:=C_{1}\eta+C_{2}\psi
, where
C_{1}\in C^{s+1,1}(G_{2R})
,
C_{2}\in C^{s,1}(G_{2R})
if
s\geq 0
,
C_{2}\in L^{\infty}(G_{2R})
for
s=-1
;

•

e^{I\alpha}=C_{3}^{IJ\alpha}\varphi_{J}+C_{4}^{IJ\alpha}\psi_{J}:=C_{3}\varphi+C_{4}\psi
, where
C_{3},C_{4}\in C^{s+1,1}(G_{2R})
;

In particular, for all
s\geq-1
the coefficients
a^{IJ\alpha\beta}
, the coefficients of
\varphi
,
\psi
in
e
, the coefficients of
\eta
in
f
are Lipschitz,

(7.2)

\begin{gathered}|a^{IJ\alpha\beta}(x)-a^{IJ\alpha\beta}(0)|\leq CR\quad\text{for}\quad x\in G_{2R},\\
|f|\leq C(|\eta|+|\psi|),\quad|e|\leq C(|\varphi|+|\psi|)\end{gathered}

with some universal constants which will be the same for any
\varepsilon
-atlas from our collection. For
s\geq 0
there holds

(7.3)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\eta\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}),

\displaystyle\|e\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\varphi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}).

In the Euclidean metric for a function
\zeta
with compact support in
G_{2R}
(and vanishing tangential/ normal part on
\{x_{n}=0\}
in the boundary coordinate patch the Gaffney formula holds

(7.4)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{G_{2R}}a^{IJ\alpha\beta}(0)\zeta_{I,\alpha}\omega_{J,\beta}\,dx

\displaystyle=(d\omega,d\zeta)_{0}+(\delta_{0}\omega,\delta_{0}\zeta)_{0}

\displaystyle=(\nabla\omega,\nabla\zeta)_{0;G_{2R}}:=\int\limits_{G_{2R}}\delta^{IJ}\delta^{\alpha\beta}\omega_{J,\beta}\zeta_{I,\alpha}\,dx.

Here
\delta_{0}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{0}
stand for the codifferential and the scalar product in the standard Euclidean metric, and vanishing of the boundary integral here is equivalent to the relation
a^{IJ\alpha n}(0)\omega_{J}\zeta_{I}z_{\alpha}-a^{IJn\beta}(0)\omega_{J}\zeta_{I}z_{\beta}=0
on
x^{n}=0
if
t\omega=t\zeta=0
or
n\omega=n\zeta=0
.

Further we shall use the following notation. Let

(7.5)

\begin{gathered}(\nabla\zeta)_{I\alpha}=\zeta_{I,\alpha},\quad(a\nabla\omega)^{I\alpha}=a^{IJ\alpha\beta}\omega_{J,\beta},\quad\nabla\omega:\nabla\zeta=\delta^{IJ}\delta^{\alpha\beta}\omega_{J,\beta}\zeta_{I,\alpha},\\
(b\omega)^{I\alpha}=b^{IJ\alpha}\omega_{J},\quad(b^{*}\nabla\omega)^{I}=b^{*IJ\beta}\omega_{J,\beta},\quad(c\omega)^{I}=c^{IJ}\omega_{J},\end{gathered}

Products of such expressions are understood in the usual way as contractions over repeated lower and upper indices:

a\nabla\omega\nabla\zeta=a^{IJ\alpha\beta}\omega_{J,\beta}\zeta_{I,\alpha},\quad b\omega\nabla\zeta=b^{IJ\alpha}\omega_{J}\zeta_{I,\alpha},\quad b^{*}\nabla\omega=b^{*IJ\beta}\omega_{J,\beta}\zeta_{I},

and so forth. We shall reserve the indices
J
,
\beta
to
\omega
and its localized version
\Omega
below, and
I,\alpha
to test functions. Rewrite now (
7.1
) as

(7.6)

\int\limits_{G_{2R}}((a\nabla\omega+b\omega+e)\nabla\zeta+(b^{*}\nabla\omega+c\omega+f)\zeta)\,dx=0.

Further we assume that
\omega
is a homogeneous form of degree
r
solving one of the three BVPs formulated above (full Dirichlet data/ Dirichlet/ Neumann) for (
4.5
) and in a local coordinate chart in (
7.1
) (equivalently (
7.6
)) the form
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
, and the data
e,f\in L^{p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
. We assume that
M
is at least of the class
C^{s+2,1}
,
s\geq-1
.

7.2.
Potentials for the Hodge Laplacian

In the interior patches for
F=F^{I}
and
E=E^{I\alpha}
we introduce the volume potentials
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
as

(\mathcal{P}[F])_{I}=P[F^{I}],\quad(\mathcal{Q}[E])_{I}=Q[E^{I}]=Q_{\alpha}[E^{I\alpha}]

where the volume potentials
P
,
Q
are defined in Section
3.4
.

In the case of boundary patches we use the following extensions of densities of these potentials, which correspond to solving the Dirichlet/Neumann problem in the half-space
x^{n}>0
by odd/even reflection. Using the notation of Section
3.5
where the parameter
\rho=1
, that is the potentials corresponding to the standard Laplace operator are used, we define

Let

•

g_{I}(x,y)=g_{D}(x,y)
for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
;

•

g_{I}(x,y)=g_{N}(x,y)
for
I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}
;

•

g_{I\alpha}(x,y)=\frac{\partial g_{D}(x,y)}{\partial y^{\alpha}}
for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
;

•

g_{I\alpha}(x,y)=\frac{\partial g_{N}(x,y)}{\partial y^{\alpha}}
for
I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}
;

•

\mathcal{E}^{I}=\mathcal{E}^{D}
for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
,;

•

\mathcal{E}^{I}=\mathcal{E}^{N}
for
I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}
;

•

\mathcal{E}^{I\alpha}=\mathcal{E}^{D}
if
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
and
\alpha<n
or
I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}
and
\alpha=n
;

•

\mathcal{E}^{I\alpha}=\mathcal{E}^{D}
if
I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}
and
\alpha=n
or
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
and
\alpha=n
.

Then, for boundary patches we define (in the following formula there is no summation in
I
)

(7.7)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}[F])_{I}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\delta_{IJ}g_{I}(x,y)F^{J}(y)\,dy=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{0}(x-y)\mathcal{E}^{I}[F^{I}](y)\,dy,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}[E])_{I}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\delta_{IJ}g_{I\alpha}(x,y)E^{J\alpha}(y)\,dy=-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{0,\alpha}(x-y)\mathcal{E}^{I\alpha}[E^{I\alpha}](y)\,dy.

In the notation of Section
3.5
this means

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}[F])_{I}

\displaystyle=P^{D}[F^{I}],\quad I\in\mathcal{I}_{0},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}[F])_{I}

\displaystyle=P^{N}[F^{I}],\quad I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}[E])_{I}

\displaystyle=Q^{D}[E^{I}]=Q^{D}_{\alpha}[E^{I\alpha}],\quad I\in\mathcal{I}_{0},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}[E])_{I}

\displaystyle=Q^{N}[E^{I}]=Q^{N}_{\alpha}[E^{I\alpha}],\quad I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}.

Note that

(1)

for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0},
the potential
(\mathcal{P}[F])_{I}
solves the Dirichlet problem

\triangle u=F^{I}\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\qquad u=0\text{ on }x_{n}=0;

(2)

for
I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0},
the potential
(\mathcal{P}[F])_{I}
solves the Neumann problem

\triangle u=F^{I}\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\qquad\partial u/\partial x_{n}=0\text{ on }x_{n}=0;

(3)

for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0},
the potential
(\mathcal{Q}[E])_{I}
solves the Dirichlet problem

\triangle u=-(E^{I\alpha})_{x^{\alpha}}\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\qquad u=0\text{ on }x_{n}=0;

(4)

for
I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0},
the potential
(\mathcal{Q}[E])_{I}
solves the Neumann problem

\triangle u=-(E^{I\alpha})_{x^{\alpha}}\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\qquad\partial u/\partial x^{n}+E^{In}=0\text{ on }x^{n}=0.

For
\omega=\{\omega_{I}\}
we denote (for functions the norm
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{s,p,G_{2R}}
was introduced in Sections
3.4
,
3.5
)

\|\omega\|^{*}_{s,p,G_{2R}}=\sum_{I}\|\omega_{I}\|^{*}_{s,p,G_{2R}}.

If
n=2
we also need the following notation. For
F=F^{I}
introduce the set of constants
c(F;G_{2R})=\{c_{I}(F,G_{2R})\}
by

(7.8)

c_{I}(F;G_{2R})=\begin{cases}c(F^{I};G_{2R})\quad\text{if}\quad I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0},\\
0,\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Here the constants
c(f;G_{2R})
are introduced by the expressions in (
3.3
), (
3.6
).

From Lemma
3.11
and Lemma
3.14
we get

Lemma 7.1
.

For
F=F^{I}
and
E=E^{I\alpha}
with support in
\widetilde{G}_{2R}
there holds

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}[E]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|E\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[F]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))R\|F\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[F]\|^{*}_{1,\varkappa p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|F\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{Q}[E],\mathcal{P}[F]\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
. If
n=2
the estimates for
\mathcal{P}[F]
are valid if
\mathcal{P}[F]
is replaced by
\mathcal{P}[F]-c(F;G_{2R})
.

From Corollary
3.13
and Lemma
3.16
we get

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
F\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, with support in
\widetilde{G}_{2R}
. Then

\|\mathcal{P}[F]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq C\|F\|_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Let
E\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, with support in
G_{2R}
. Then

\|\mathcal{Q}[E]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq C\|E\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}^{*}.

Here
C=C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p)).
If
n=2
the estimates for
\mathcal{P}[F]
are valid if
\mathcal{P}[F]
is replaced by
\mathcal{P}[F]-c(F;G_{2R})
.

7.3.
Local a priori estimates

Let
\xi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{7R/4})
be such that
\xi=1
on
B_{R}
,
0\leq\xi\leq 1
,
|\nabla\xi|\leq C(n)R^{-1}
, and let
\xi_{*}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{2R})
be such that
0\leq\xi_{*}\leq 1
,
\xi_{*}=1
on
B_{7R/4}
, and
|\nabla\xi_{*}|\leq C(n)R^{-1}
.

Using in (
4.5
) test form
\xi\zeta
instead of
\zeta
one obtains the following localized relation:

(7.9)

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{D}(\omega\xi,\zeta)=(\widetilde{\varphi},d\zeta)+(\widetilde{\psi},\delta\zeta)+(\widetilde{\eta},\zeta),\\
\widetilde{\varphi}=\xi\varphi+d\xi\wedge\omega,\quad\widetilde{\psi}=\xi\psi-d\xi\lrcorner\omega,\\
\widetilde{\eta}=\xi\eta+d\xi\wedge\delta\omega-d\xi\lrcorner d\omega+d\xi\lrcorner\varphi-d\xi\wedge\psi.\end{gathered}

Denote

(7.10)

\begin{gathered}\Omega=\omega\xi,\quad E=\xi e-a\omega\nabla\xi,\quad F=\xi f+e\nabla\xi+a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi+(b-b^{*})\omega\nabla\xi,\\
(a\omega\nabla\xi)^{I\alpha}=a^{IJ\alpha\beta}\xi_{,\beta}\omega_{J},\quad(a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi)^{I}=\xi_{,\alpha}a^{IJ\alpha\beta}\omega_{J,\beta},\\
((b-b^{*})\omega\nabla\xi)^{I}=(b^{IJ\alpha}-b^{*IJ\alpha})\xi_{,\alpha}\omega_{J},\quad(e\nabla\xi)^{I}=e^{I\alpha}\xi_{,\alpha}.\end{gathered}

Recalling also the notation (
7.5
), for
\Omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
from (
7.1
) with
\zeta
replaced by
\xi\zeta
, and using (
7.4
), we get the formulation for (
7.9
) in coordinates:

(7.11)

\int\limits_{G_{2R}}\left[\nabla\Omega:\nabla\zeta+(\xi_{*}[(a(\cdot)-a(0))\nabla\Omega+b\Omega]+E)\nabla\zeta\right.\\
+\left.(\xi_{*}[b^{*}\nabla\Omega+c\Omega]+F)\zeta\right]\,dx=0,

where
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(X)
,
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
G_{2R}=B_{2R}
and
X=\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}
if
G_{2R}=\overline{B}_{2R}^{+}
,
\zeta_{I}=0
on
\{x^{n}=0\}
for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
. The relation between some of the terms in the RHS of (
7.9
) and (
7.10
)-(
7.11
) is

\displaystyle(d\xi\lrcorner d\omega-d\xi\wedge\delta\omega,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{G_{2R}}(a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi+b\omega\nabla\xi)\zeta\,dx

\displaystyle-(d\xi\wedge\omega,d\zeta)+(d\xi\lrcorner\omega,\delta\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{G_{2R}}[-(a\omega\nabla\xi)\nabla\zeta-(b^{*}\omega\nabla\xi)\zeta]\,dx,

\displaystyle(-d\xi\lrcorner\varphi+d\xi\wedge\psi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{G_{2R}}(e\nabla\xi)\zeta\,dx.

Note that there holds

(7.12)

\int\limits_{G_{2R}}[\xi_{*}(b^{*}\nabla\Omega+c\Omega)+F]^{K}\,dx=\quad\text{for}\quad K\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}.

To check this relation it is sufficient to take the test form with coordinates
\zeta_{I}=\delta_{IK}
in (
7.11
).

Recall that in notation of Section
7.1

f=C_{1}\eta+C_{2}\psi,\quad e=C_{3}\varphi+C_{4}\psi,

and the regularity of the coefficients is

•

a,C_{1},C_{3},C_{4}\in C^{s+1,1}(G_{2R})
,

•

b,b^{*},c,C_{2}\in C^{s,1}(G_{2R})
if
s\geq 0
and
b,b^{*},c,C_{2}\in L^{\infty}(G_{2R})
if
s=-1
.

The corresponding bounds of the coefficients are uniform with respect to
R
and the chosen element of the cover.

In
G_{2R}
there holds

(7.13)

\displaystyle|(a(\cdot)-a(0))\xi_{*}|

\displaystyle\leq CR,

\displaystyle|\nabla[(a(\cdot)-a(0))\xi_{*}]|,|b\xi_{*}|,|b^{*}\xi_{*}|,|c\xi_{*}|

\displaystyle\leq C,

and if
M
is at least of the class
C^{2,1}
there holds

(7.14)

|\nabla[b\xi_{*}]|,|\nabla[b^{*}\xi_{*}]|,|\nabla[c\xi_{*}]|\leq CR^{-1}.

Lemma 7.3
.

There holds

(7.15)

\Omega=\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}((a(\cdot)-a(0)\nabla\Omega+b\Omega)+E]+\mathcal{P}[\xi_{*}(b^{*}\nabla\Omega+c\Omega)+F].

Proof.

By Lemma
7.1
the expression on the right is well-defined and belongs at least to
W^{1,p_{-}}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
. Moreover, (for
n=2
this is due to (
7.12
)) the potentials on the right decay at infinity.

We shall treat the case of boundary patches, the interior case being simpler. Denote

A=\xi_{*}[a(\cdot)-a(0)\nabla\Omega+b\Omega]+E,\quad B=\xi_{*}(b^{*}\nabla\Omega+c\Omega)+F.

There holds

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}(\nabla\mathcal{Q}[A]:\nabla\zeta+A\nabla\zeta)\,dx=0,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}(\nabla\mathcal{P}[B]:\nabla\zeta+B\zeta)\,dx=0,

where the test functions
\zeta_{I}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}})
satisfy
\zeta_{I}=0
on
x_{n}=0
for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
.

Substracting these relations from (
7.11
) we get

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\nabla V:\nabla\zeta=0,

where

V:=\Omega-Q[A]-P[B]

vanishes on
x_{n}=0
for indices
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
. Therefore, the odd extension of
V_{I}
, if
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
, and even extension of
V_{I}
for
I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}
across
x_{n}=0
satisfies

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla V:\nabla\zeta\,dx=0

for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By the Weyl lemma all the functions
V_{I}
are harmonic, and since
V_{I}\to 0
as
x\to\infty
we get
V_{I}\equiv 0
.
∎

Define the operator

(7.16)

T[\Omega]=\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}((a(\cdot)-a(0))\nabla\Omega+b\Omega)]+\mathcal{P}[\xi_{*}(b^{*}\nabla\Omega+c\Omega)].

The operator
T
depends on
\xi_{*}
. The integral equation (
7.15
) rewrites as

(7.17)

\Omega-T[\Omega]=\mathcal{Q}[E]+\mathcal{P}[F].

For
n=2
this needs the following modification. We set

T^{\prime}[\Omega]=T[\Omega]-c(\xi_{*}(b^{*}\nabla\Omega+c\Omega);G_{2R}),

and instead of (
7.17
) by using (
7.12
) we work with the integral equation

(7.18)

\Omega-T^{\prime}[\Omega]=\mathcal{Q}[E]+\mathcal{P}[F]-c(F;G_{2R}).

With this modification all the estimates work in exactly the same way as for
n\geq 3
, so further we use the equation in the form (
7.17
).

7.4.
Estimates in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)

Consider the operator
T
as a mapping from
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
to the same space. We show that for sufficiently small
R>0
the operator
T
is a contraction in the norm
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

For sufficiently small
R=R(\mathrm{data})>0
there holds

(7.19)

\|T[\Omega]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\Omega\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Proof.

From Lemma
7.1
it follows that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}(a(\cdot)-a(0))\nabla\Omega]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))R\|\nabla\Omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}b\Omega]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|b\|_{\infty}\|\Omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[\xi_{*}b\nabla\Omega]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|b\|_{\infty}R\|\nabla\Omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[\xi_{*}c\Omega]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))\|c\|_{\infty}R\|\Omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Then

\|T[\Omega]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq CR\|\Omega\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

where the constant
C
depends only on
n
,
p_{-}
,
p_{+}
,
c_{\mathrm{log}}(p)
, the Lipschitz constant of
a^{IJ\alpha\beta}
, and on
\|b\|_{\infty}+\|c\|_{\infty}
. It remains to choose
R\leq 1/(2C)
.
∎

Let
R
be sufficiently small such that the estimate (
7.19
) holds for all exponents
q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{log}}(p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
be a solution of (
7.1
). Let
E\in L^{p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
and
F\in L^{p_{-}}(G_{2R})
in (
7.10
) be such that
\mathcal{P}[F]\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
. Then we have
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{3R/2})
and

\|\xi\omega\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq 2(\|\mathcal{Q}[E]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\mathcal{P}[F]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

Proof.

In
W^{1,p_{-}}(G_{2R})
for
\Omega=\omega\xi
defined in (
7.10
) there holds (
7.17
) and so

(7.20)

\Omega=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}T^{j}(\mathcal{Q}[E]+\mathcal{P}[F]).

But the series in the RHS converge also in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
. Thus
\Omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
and so
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{R})
with the required estimate.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Assume
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
be a solution to (
7.1
) with
e,f\in L^{p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{3R/2})
. Moreover,

(7.21)

\|\omega\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{3R/2}}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\omega\|^{*}_{1,p_{-},G_{3R/2}}+\|e\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|f\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

Proof.

Let
\varkappa=n/(n-1)
and
l_{0}
be the minimum integer such that
\varkappa^{l}p_{-}\geq p(\cdot)
. Denote

q_{l}(x)=\min(\varkappa^{l}p_{-},p(x)),\quad x\in G_{2R}.

Clearly
l_{0}\leq 1+\log_{\varkappa}(p_{+}/p_{-})
. Define

R_{l}=\frac{7R}{4}-\frac{Rl}{4l_{0}},\quad l=0,\ldots,l_{0},\quad\widetilde{R}_{l}=\frac{R_{l-1}+R_{l}}{2}

Let the functions
\xi_{l}
,
l=1,\ldots,l_{0}
be such that
\xi_{l}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{\widetilde{R}_{l}})
,
0\leq\xi_{l}\leq 1
,
\xi_{l}=1
on
B_{R_{l}}
,
R|\nabla\xi_{l}|\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+})
. Let the functions
\widetilde{\xi}_{l}
,
l=1,\ldots,l_{0}
be such that
\widetilde{\xi}_{l}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{R_{l-1}})
,
0\leq\widetilde{\xi}_{l}\leq 1
,
\widetilde{\xi}_{l}=1
on
B_{\widetilde{R}_{l}}
,
R|\nabla\widetilde{\xi}_{l}|\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+})
. Let
E_{l}
and
F_{l}
be defined according to (
7.10
) with
\xi=\xi_{l}
:

E_{l}=\xi_{l}e-a\omega\nabla\xi_{l},\quad F_{l}=\xi_{l}f+e\nabla\xi_{l}+a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi_{l}+(b-b^{*})\omega\nabla\xi_{l}.

Let
\omega\in W^{1,q_{l-1}(\cdot)}(G_{R_{l-1}})
. By the Sobolev embedding of Corollary
3.6
and the Hölder inequality we get

\displaystyle\|\omega\nabla\xi_{l}\|_{q_{l}(\cdot),G_{R_{l-1}}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+})R^{-1}\|\omega\widetilde{\xi}_{l}\|_{q_{l}(\cdot),G_{R_{l-1}}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p_{-},p_{+},c_{\mathrm{log}}(p))R^{-1}R^{n/p_{-}^{\prime}}\|\omega\|^{*}_{1,q_{l-1}(\cdot),G_{R_{l-1}}}.

Now Lemma
7.1
and the Hölder inequality yield

\|\mathcal{Q}[a\omega\nabla\xi_{l}]\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\mathcal{P}[a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi_{l}]\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\mathcal{P}[(b-b^{*})\omega\nabla\xi_{l}]\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{2R}}\\
\leq C(\mathrm{data})R^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{1,q_{l-1}(\cdot),G_{R_{l}}}

and

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{l}e]\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|e\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[\xi_{l}f]\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|f\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[e\nabla\xi_{l}]\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|e\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Thus from Lemma
7.5
we get

\displaystyle\|\omega\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{R_{l}}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\mathcal{Q}[E_{l}]\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{2R}}+2\|\mathcal{P}[F_{l}]\|^{*}_{1,q_{l}(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|e\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|f\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+R^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{1,q_{l-1}(\cdot),G_{R_{l}}}).

Iterating this inequality we obtain
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{3R/2})
and the required estimate.
∎

The estimate we obtain in Lemma
7.6
is very rough, with “bad” constant, but sufficient for our needs.

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
\omega\in W_{*}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
*\in\{T,N,0\}
be a solution to (
4.5
) (with
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
) where
\eta,\varphi,\psi\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
. Moreover,

(7.22)

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\omega\|_{W^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)}

\displaystyle+C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}).

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove this for
\omega
being a homogeneous form of degree
r
. Let
(V_{\alpha},\widetilde{h}_{\alpha})
be an
R
-atlas with sufficiently small
R
such that in each
G_{2R}=\widetilde{h}_{\alpha}(V_{\alpha})
the statement of Lemma
7.6
holds. Using estimate of Lemma
7.6
and (
7.2
) we get

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{R})}\leq C

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{W^{1,p_{-}}(G_{2R})}

\displaystyle+C(\|\eta\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})}).

Since
\widetilde{h}_{\alpha}^{-1}(G_{R})
cover
M
the claim easily follows.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

The dependence on
\nabla\omega
in
F
(see (
7.10
)) arises only via
d\omega
and
\delta\omega
, thus

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi+b\omega\nabla\xi]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C(\mathrm{data})R\|a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi+b\omega\nabla\xi\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|d\omega\|^{*}_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\delta\omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

That is, the term
\|\omega\|_{1,p_{-},M}
on the RHS of the estimate (
7.22
) can be replaced by
\|d\omega\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\omega\|_{p_{-},M}
without direct use of the Gaffney inequality.

Together with the standard Gaffney inequality Corollary
7.7
provides the following a priori estimate.

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
p_{-}\geq 2
,
\omega\in W_{*}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
*\in\{T,N,0\}
be a solution to (
4.5
) (with
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
) where
\eta,\varphi,\psi\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
. Moreover,

(7.23)

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\omega\|_{1,M}

\displaystyle+C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{p(\cdot),M}).

Proof.

In this case we can use
\omega
itself as the test form in (
4.5
). This gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega)=(\eta,\omega)+(\varphi,d\omega)+(\psi,d\omega)

\displaystyle\leq\|\eta\|_{2,M}\|\omega\|_{2,M}+\|\varphi\|_{2,M}\|d\omega\|_{2,M}+\|\psi\|_{2,M}\|\delta\omega\|_{2,M}.

Thus

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega)\leq\|\eta\|_{2,M}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{2,M}^{2}+2\|\varphi\|_{2,M}^{2}+2\|\psi\|_{2,M}^{2}.

By the classical Gaffney inequality we have

\|\omega\|_{1,2,M}^{2}\leq C(\mathcal{D}(\omega,\omega)+\|\omega\|_{2,M}^{2})\leq C(\|\eta\|_{2,M}^{2}+\|\varphi\|_{2,M}^{2}+\|\psi\|_{2,M}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{2,M}^{2}).

It remains to use Corollary
7.7
with
p_{-}=2
, the Hölder inequality, and interpolation to replace
\|\omega\|_{2,M}
by
\|\omega\|_{1,M}
.
∎

7.5.
Estimates in
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)

In this section,
M
will be at least of the class
C^{s+2,1}
,
s\geq 0
.

In (
7.10
) choose the cut-off function
\xi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{7R/4})
such that
\xi=1
on
B_{R}
,
0\leq\xi\leq 1
,
|\nabla^{k}\xi|\leq C(n,s)R^{-k}
,
k\leq s+2
and
\xi_{*}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{2R})
such that
0\leq\xi_{*}\leq 1
and
\xi_{*}=1
on
B_{7R/4}
, and
\sum_{k=0}^{s+2}R^{k}|\nabla^{k}\xi_{*}|\leq C(n,s)
.

Consider the operator
T
from (
7.16
) as a mapping from
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
to the same space. Let us show that for sufficiently small
R
the operator
T
is a contraction in the norm
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}
.

Denote

\|\gamma\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}=\sum_{k=0}^{s}R^{k}\|\nabla^{k}\gamma\|_{\infty,G_{2R}}.

By definition,
\|\xi_{*}\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}\leq C_{*}(n)

Using the Leibnitz formula one easily sees that

Lemma 7.10
.

For
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in C^{s-1,1}(G_{2R})
there holds

\|\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}\leq C(n,s)\|\gamma_{1}\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}\|\gamma_{1}\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}.

For
\gamma\in C^{s-1,1}(G_{2R})
there holds

\|\xi_{*}\gamma\Omega\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq\|\gamma\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}C(n,s)\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Let
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 7.11
.

For sufficiently small
R=R(\mathrm{data},s)
there holds

(7.24)

\|T[\Omega]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Proof.

Recall that in the notation of (
7.16
),

T[\Omega]=\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}a((\cdot)-a(0))\nabla\Omega+\xi_{*}b\Omega]+\mathcal{P}[\xi_{*}b^{*}\nabla\Omega+\xi_{*}c\Omega].

Since
a\in C^{s+1,1}
,
b,c\in C^{s,1}
,
s\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\|\xi_{*}a((\cdot)-a(0))\nabla\Omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s)\|\nabla a\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}R\|\nabla\Omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s)\|\nabla a\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}R\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot,G_{2R})},

\displaystyle\|\xi_{*}b\Omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s)\|b\|_{s+1,\infty,G_{2R}}\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s)\|b\|_{s+1,\infty,G_{2R}}R\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\xi_{*}b^{*}\nabla\Omega\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s)\|b^{*}\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}\|\nabla\Omega\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s)\|b^{*}\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}R\|\nabla\Omega\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\xi_{*}c\Omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s)\|c\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s)\|c\|_{s,\infty,G_{2R}}R^{2}\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Lemma
7.2
then gives

\displaystyle\|T[\Omega]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\|\xi_{*}a((\cdot)-a(0))\nabla\Omega+\xi_{*}b\Omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\xi_{*}b^{*}\nabla\Omega+\xi_{*}c\Omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq CR\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

The constant
C
here depends only on
n
,
p(\cdot)
(via
p_{-}
,
p_{+}
,
c_{\mathrm{log}}p
), the corresponding bounds for the coefficients. It remains to choose
R\leq 1/(2C)
. ∎

By Lemma
7.11
, we can choose
R>0
sufficiently small such that the mapping
T
is a contraction, both in
W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
and
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R}),
with the norm not exceeding
1/2
:

(7.25)

\|T\Omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\Omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}},\quad k=s+1,s+2.

Corollary 7.12
.

Let
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
be a solution of (
7.1
) where
f\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
and
e\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(G_{R})
and

\|\omega\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{R}}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|f\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|e\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+R^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

Proof.

By Lemmas
7.2
,
7.10
we get

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}[E]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\xi e-a\omega\nabla\xi\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq CR^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+C\|e\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[F]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\xi f+e\nabla\xi+a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi+(b-b^{*})\omega\nabla\xi\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|^{*}_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+C\|e\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot)}+CR^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

By (
7.25
) equation (
7.17
) is uniquely solvable in
W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
, and the solution is given by (
7.20
). The series converge also in
W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
and

\displaystyle\|\Omega\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\mathcal{Q}[E]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+2\|\mathcal{P}[F]\|^{*}_{s+2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f\|_{s,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|e\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+R^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{s+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

It remains to use that
\omega=\Omega
in
G_{R}
.
∎

Theorem 7.13
.

Let
\omega
be a
W_{*}^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
*\in\{T,N,0\}
, solution of (
4.5
), where
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
, with
\eta\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

\|\omega\|_{W^{s+2,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}\\
\leq C(\|\eta\|_{W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\varphi\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\psi\|_{W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)}+\|\omega\|_{L^{1}(M,\Lambda)})

where
C=C(\mathrm{data},s)
.

Proof.

By Corollary
7.7
we have
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Assume without loss that
\omega
is a homogeneous even form of degree
r
.

We argue by induction in
s
. Let
1\leq k\leq s+1
and
\omega\in W^{k,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then in a sufficiently fine
\varepsilon
-atlas by Corollary
7.12
we have

\|\omega\|_{k+1,p(\cdot),G_{R}}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

Since the neighborhoods
\widetilde{h}_{\alpha}^{-1}(G_{R})
cover
M
we get

(7.26)

\|\omega\|_{k+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}).

The interpolation inequality of Lemma
3.9
then gives

\|\omega\|_{k+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

When
k
reaches
s+1
we get the required estimate.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

The dependence on
\nabla\omega
in
F
(see (
7.10
)) arises only via
d\omega
and
\delta\omega
, thus for
k=0,\ldots,s+1
there holds

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi+b\omega\nabla\xi]\|^{*}_{k+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C(\mathrm{data},k)R\|a\nabla\omega\nabla\xi+b\omega\nabla\xi\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|d\omega\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\delta\omega\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\omega\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

That is, the term
\|\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}
on the RHS in the intermediate estimate (
7.26
) can be replaced by
\|d\omega\|_{k-1,p(\cdot,M)}+\|\delta\omega\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}
.

7.6.
Gaffney inequality with natural regularity assumptions

In this Section we obtain Gaffney’s inequality in variable exponent spaces by a direct argument without using the Hodge decomposition of Section
6.4
.

Theorem 7.15
.
