Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+1,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
,
\omega\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
d\omega,\delta\omega\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
t\omega=0
or
n\omega=0
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

(7.27)

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{*}\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|d\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M})

where
*
is
T
in the case of
t\omega=0
and
*=N
for
n\omega=0
.

The proof of this theorem will comprise of several lemmas.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+1,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
,
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
,
*\in\{0,T,N\}
, and
d\omega,\delta\omega\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

(7.28)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|d\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

Proof.

Clearly,
\omega
satisfies (
4.5
) with
\varphi=d\omega
,
\psi=\delta\omega
,
\eta=0
, for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
. Using Lemma
7.7
we get
\omega\in W_{*}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
.

Let
\xi
be a
C^{s+1,1}
function supported in one of the coordinate neighborhoods. Then
\Omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\Omega,\zeta)=(\varphi,d\zeta)+(\psi,\delta\zeta)\quad\text{for all}\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\varphi=\xi d\omega+d\xi\wedge\omega,\quad\psi=\xi\delta\omega-d\xi\lrcorner\omega.

As above, we assume without loss that
\omega
is a homogeneous form of degree
r
. In coordinates following the notation of Section
7.3
we get a relation similar to (
7.11
):

(7.29)

\int\limits_{G_{2R}}\left[\nabla\Omega:\nabla\zeta+(\xi_{*}[(a(\cdot)-a(0))\nabla\Omega+b\Omega]+\hat{e})\nabla\zeta\right.\\
+\left.(\xi_{*}[b^{*}\nabla\Omega+c\Omega]+\hat{f})\zeta\right]\,dx=0,

where
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
, and the dependence of
e
,
f
on
\varphi
,
\psi
is described in Section
7.1
. As in Section
7.3
, we get (with the the same modification for
n=2
, see (
7.18
))

\Omega-T[\Omega]=\mathcal{P}[\hat{f}]+\mathcal{Q}[\hat{e}].

From Lemmas
7.4
,
7.11
for sufficiently small
R>0
the operator
T
acts as a contraction on
W^{k+1,p(\cdot)}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
,
k=0,\ldots,s
. Therefore

\displaystyle\|\Omega\|_{k+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\|\mathcal{P}[\hat{f}]\|_{k+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\mathcal{Q}[\hat{e}]\|_{k+1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}})

\displaystyle\leq C(\|d\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\delta\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

Since we can cover our manifold by an open cover where each element is the image of
G_{R}
, we get

\|\omega\|_{k+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|d\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}).

By iterating this inequality and using the interpolation Lemma
3.9
we finally arrive at the Gaffney type estimate (
7.28
).
∎

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
M
be of the class
C^{s+1,1}
,
s\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
,
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
,
*\in\{0,T,N\}
, and
d\omega,\delta\omega\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

(7.30)

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{*}\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|d\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}).

Proof.

We prove this for the tangential boundary condition. Assume without loss that
p_{-}\leq 2
and let
\eta\in L^{p_{-}^{\prime}}(M,\Lambda)
,
\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta=0
. Since for
\Phi=G_{D}[\eta]
we have

\|\Phi\|_{2,M}\leq C(M)(\|d\Phi\|_{2,M}+\|\delta\Phi\|_{2,M})=C(M)\sqrt{\mathcal{D}(\Phi,\Phi)},\quad\mathcal{D}(\Phi,\Phi)=(\eta,\Phi),

we get
\mathcal{D}(\Phi,\Phi)\leq C(M)(\eta,\eta)
and

\|\Phi\|_{2,M}\leq C(M)\|\eta\|_{2,M}.

By the classical Gaffney inequality,

\|\Phi\|_{1,2,M}\leq C(M)(\|d\Phi\|_{2,M}+\|\delta\Phi\|_{2,M})\leq C(M)\|\eta\|_{2,M}.

By Lemma
7.7
and Lemma
7.16
,

\|\Phi\|_{1,p_{-}^{\prime},M}\leq C(M,p_{-})\|\eta\|_{p_{-}^{\prime},M}.

Then

(\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega,\eta)=\mathcal{D}(\Phi,\omega)\leq C(M,p_{-})(\|d\omega\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{p_{-},M})\|\eta\|_{p_{-}^{\prime},M}.

It follows that for any
\zeta\in L^{p_{-}^{\prime}}(M,\Lambda)
there holds

(\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega,\zeta)\leq C(M,p_{-})(\|d\omega\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{p_{-},M})\|\zeta\|_{p_{-}^{\prime},M}.

Thus

\|\omega-\mathcal{P}_{T}\omega\|_{p_{-},M}\leq C(M,p_{-})(\|d\omega\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\delta\omega\|_{p_{-},M}).

Combined with Lemma
7.16
this yields (
7.30
).
∎

To prove Theorem
7.15
it remains to show that the existence of generalized differential and codifferential in
L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
implies that
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
. This is proved in Lemma
7.19
and Lemma
7.20
for tangential and normal boundary conditions, respectively. To prove them, we shall use the following technical lemma.

Lemma 7.18
.

(i) Let
a^{\alpha\beta},f^{\alpha},w\in L^{p_{-}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the function
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with compact support satisfy

(7.31)

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u\triangle\varphi\,dx=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}[a^{\alpha\beta}\varphi_{,\alpha\beta}+f^{\alpha}\varphi_{,\alpha}+w\varphi]\,dx

for all
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then

(7.32)

u=(P[a^{\alpha\beta}])_{,\alpha\beta}-(P[f^{\alpha}])_{,\alpha}+P[w].

If
n=2
one additionally requires that the integral of
w
over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is zero.

(ii) Let
a_{\alpha\beta},f^{\alpha},w\in L^{p_{-}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
and the function
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
with compact support in
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}
satisfy

(7.33)

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}u\triangle\varphi\,dx=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}[a^{\alpha\beta}\varphi_{,\alpha\beta}+f^{\alpha}\varphi_{,\alpha}+w\varphi]\,dx

for all
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}})
satisfying
\varphi=0
on
\{x^{n}=0\}
. Then

(7.34)

\displaystyle u=\begin{aligned} P^{D}[w]&-\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n-1}(P^{D}[f^{\alpha}])_{,\alpha}-(P^{N}[f^{n}])_{,n}\\
&+\sum_{\alpha,\beta<n}(P^{D}[a^{\alpha\beta}])_{,\alpha\beta}+(P^{D}[a^{nn}])_{,nn}\\
&\qquad+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n-1}(P^{N}[a^{\alpha n}])_{,\alpha n}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{n-1}(P^{N}[a^{n\beta}])_{,n\beta}.\end{aligned}

(iii) If the integral identity (
7.33
) holds for all
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}})
satisfying
\varphi_{,n}=0
on
\{x^{n}=0\}
then

(7.35)

\displaystyle u=\begin{aligned} P^{N}[w]&-\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n-1}(P^{N}[f^{\alpha}])_{,\alpha}-(P^{D}[f^{n}])_{,n}\\
&+\sum_{\alpha,\beta<n}(P^{N}[a^{\alpha\beta}])_{,\alpha\beta}+(P^{N}[a^{nn}])_{,nn}\\
&\qquad+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n-1}(P^{D}[a^{\alpha n}])_{,\alpha n}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{n-1}(P^{D}[a^{n\beta}])_{,n\beta}.\end{aligned}

If
n=2
this holds if the integral of
w
over
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
is zero.

Proof.

To prove the first statement consider the function
v
defined by the right-hand side of (
7.32
). This function satisfies relation (
7.31
), thus by the Weyl lemma the difference
u-v
is harmonic. Since it vanishes at infinity,
u=v
.

The second statement follows by considering the odd extension of
u
across
x^{n}=0
, which satisfies (
7.31
) for all
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
a^{\alpha\beta}
obtained by odd extension if
\alpha,\beta<n
or
\alpha=\beta=n
and even extension otherwise,
f^{\alpha}
obtained by odd extension if
\alpha<n
and by even extension if
\alpha=n
, and
w
obtained by the odd extension.

The third statement follows by considering the even extension of
u
across
x^{n}=0
, which satisfies (
7.31
) for all
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
a^{\alpha\beta}
obtained by even extension if
\alpha,\beta<n
or
\alpha=\beta=n
and odd extension otherwise,
f^{\alpha}
obtained by even extension if
\alpha<n
and by odd extension if
\alpha=n
, and
w
obtained by the even extension.
∎

In (
7.32
), (
7.34
), and (
7.35
) the terms on the RHS corresponding to
f
can be written as
Q[f]
,
Q^{D}[f]
, and
Q^{N}[f]
, respectively.

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
M\in C^{1,1}
and
\omega,d\omega,\delta\omega\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
t\omega=0
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
.

Proof.

By definition, for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}(M,\Lambda)
and
\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
there holds

(\omega,\delta\varphi)=(f,\varphi),\quad(\omega,d\psi)=(v,\psi),

where
f,v\in L^{p}(M,\Lambda)
.

Therefore,

(7.36)

(\omega,\delta\varphi)+(\omega,d\psi)=(f,\varphi)+(v,\psi),

for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}(M,\Lambda)
,
\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
.

Now, replacing the test forms
\varphi
and
\psi
by
\xi\varphi,\xi\psi
, where
\xi
is an arbitrary Lipschitz function with support in one of coordinate charts, we get

(\xi\omega,\delta\varphi+d\psi)=(\xi f+d\xi\wedge\omega,\varphi)+(\xi v-d\xi\lrcorner\omega,\psi),

or

(7.37)

\begin{gathered}(\Omega,\delta\varphi+d\psi)=(\widetilde{f},\varphi)+(\widetilde{v},\psi),\\
\Omega=\xi\omega,\quad\widetilde{f}=\xi f+d\xi\wedge\omega,\quad\widetilde{v}=\xi v-d\xi\lrcorner\omega.\end{gathered}

We shall prove that
\Omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
.

We can assume that
\omega
is a homogeneous form of degree
r
. Then in coordinates the relation (
7.37
) takes the form

(7.38)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{G_{2R}}G^{IJ}_{r}\Omega_{I}

\displaystyle[(\delta\varphi)_{J}+(d\psi)_{J}]\sqrt{g}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{G_{2R}}(G^{IJ}_{r+1}\widetilde{f}_{I}\varphi_{J}+G^{IJ}_{r-1}\widetilde{v}_{I}\psi_{J})\sqrt{g}\,dx.

Here
\varphi
,
\psi
are Lipshitz on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}
in boundary patches) with
\psi_{I}=0
on
\{x_{n}=0\}
if
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
(that is if
I
does not contain
n
). Further we work in a boundary coordinate system, the interior case being simpler.

Since
\Omega=0
outside
G_{2R}
, relation (
7.38
) can be extended to the whole
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}G^{IJ}_{r}\Omega_{I}

\displaystyle[(\delta\varphi)_{J}+(d\psi)_{J}]\sqrt{g}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}(G^{IJ}_{r+1}\widetilde{f}_{I}\varphi_{J}+G^{IJ}_{r-1}\widetilde{v}_{I}\psi_{J})\sqrt{g}\,dx.

Let
W=W_{I}dx^{I}
be a smooth form in with compact support in
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}
such that (for boundary coordinate charts)

(7.39)

W_{I}=0,\quad I\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\quad\frac{\partial W_{I}}{\partial x^{n}}=0,\quad I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}.

Recall that in this case
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
if it does not contain
n
.

Let

(7.40)

\varphi=dW,\quad\psi=\delta_{0}W,

where
\delta_{0}
is the codifferential operator in standard Euclidian metric:

\psi_{I}=-\frac{\partial W_{jI}}{\partial x^{j}}.

Then
t\psi=0
, and we have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}G^{IJ}_{r}\Omega_{I}

\displaystyle[(\delta dW)_{J}+(d\delta_{0}W)_{J}]\sqrt{g}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}(G^{IJ}_{r+1}\widetilde{f}_{I}(dW)_{J}+G^{IJ}_{r-1}\widetilde{v}_{I}(\delta_{0}W)_{J})\sqrt{g}\,dx.

By our assumption on the admissible coordinate system,
G^{IJ}(0)=\delta^{IJ}
,
\sqrt{g}(0)=1
. Using that
\delta_{0}d+d\delta_{0}=-\triangle
rewrite the relation above as

\displaystyle-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\Omega_{I}\triangle W_{I}\,dx=\begin{aligned} \int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}&(G^{IJ}_{r+1}\widetilde{f}_{I}(dW)_{J}+G^{IJ}_{r-1}\widetilde{v}_{I}(\delta_{0}W)_{J})\sqrt{g}\,dx\\
&+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\xi_{*}G^{IJ}_{r}\Omega_{I}[(\delta_{0}-\delta)dW]_{J}\,\sqrt{g}\,dx\\
&\qquad\qquad-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\xi_{*}[G^{IJ}_{r}(0)-G_{r}^{IJ}\sqrt{g}]\Omega_{I}\triangle W_{J}\,dx.\end{aligned}

Rewrite this as

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\Omega_{I}\triangle W_{I}\,dx=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}

\displaystyle F^{I\beta}W_{I,\beta}dx

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\xi_{*}\Omega_{J}(A^{IJ\alpha\beta}W_{I,\alpha\beta}+B^{IJ\alpha}W_{I,\alpha})\,dx,

where by the Lipschitz continuity of the metric on
G_{2R}
we have

(7.41)

|A^{IJ\alpha\beta}|\leq CR,\quad|B^{IJ\alpha}|,|(A^{IJ\alpha\beta})_{,\gamma}|\leq C

with
C
independent of
R
. Here
F^{I\beta}
are linear combinations of
\widetilde{f}_{I}
,
\widetilde{v}_{I}
with Lipchitz continuous coefficients which depend only on the metric:

F^{I\beta}W_{I,\beta}=-G^{IJ}_{r+1}\widetilde{f}_{I}(dW)_{J}-G^{IJ}_{r-1}\widetilde{v}_{I}(\delta_{0}W)_{J}.

Denote the terms on the RHS of (
7.34
) and (
7.35
) corresponding to
a^{\alpha\beta}
by
H_{\alpha\beta}[a]
,
H_{\alpha\beta}^{D}[a]
, and
H_{\alpha\beta}^{N}[a]
, respectively. Then by Lemma
7.18
we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{I}

\displaystyle=Q_{\alpha}^{D}[F^{I\alpha}+B^{IJ\alpha}\xi_{*}\Omega_{J}]+H_{\alpha\beta}^{D}[\xi_{*}A^{IJ\alpha\beta}\Omega_{J}],\qquad I\in\mathcal{I}_{0},

\displaystyle\Omega_{I}

\displaystyle=Q_{\alpha}^{N}[F^{I\alpha}+B^{IJ\alpha}\xi_{*}\Omega_{J}]+H_{\alpha\beta}^{N}[\xi_{*}A^{IJ\alpha\beta}\Omega_{J}],\qquad I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}.

We abbreviate this to

\Omega=\mathcal{Q}[F]+\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}B\Omega]+\mathcal{H}[\xi_{*}A\Omega].

Consider the operator

\mathcal{T}_{0}[\Omega]=\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}B\Omega]+\mathcal{H}[\xi_{*}A\Omega]

on
L^{p}(G_{2R})
. By Lemmas
3.14
,
3.15
and using (
7.41
) we get

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}B\Omega]\|_{p_{-},G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq CR\|\Omega\|_{p_{-},G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{H}[\xi_{*}A\Omega]\|_{p_{-},G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq CR\|\Omega\|_{p_{-},G_{2R}}.

Thus

\|\mathcal{T}_{0}[\Omega]\|_{p_{-},G_{2R}}\leq C(M,p_{-})R\|\Omega\|_{p_{-},G_{2R}}.

For sufficiently small
R
the operator
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is a contraction on
L^{p_{-}}(G_{2R})
with the norm less than
1/2
, and there holds

(7.42)

\Omega=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{0})^{j}\mathcal{Q}[F].

Now, using the same Lemmas
3.14
,
3.15
and estimate (
7.41
) we have

(7.43)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}B\Omega]\|^{*}_{1,p_{-},G_{2R}}&\leq C\|\Omega\|_{p_{-},G_{2R}}\leq CR\|\Omega\|^{*}_{1,p_{-},G_{2R}},\\
\|\mathcal{H}[\xi_{*}A\Omega]\|_{1,p_{-},G_{2R}}&\leq CR\|\xi_{*}A\Omega\|^{*}_{1,p_{-},G_{2R}}\leq CR\|\Omega\|^{*}_{1,p_{-},G_{2R}},\end{aligned}

which implies

\|\mathcal{T}_{0}[u]\|^{*}_{1,p_{-},G_{2R}}\leq CR\|u\|^{*}_{1,p_{-},G_{2R}}.

For sufficiently small
R>0
the operator
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is also a contraction on
W^{1,p_{-}}(G_{2R})
with the norm less than
1/2
. Thus, the series in (
7.42
) converge also in
W^{1,p_{-}}(G_{2R})
and we get
\Omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(G_{2R})
.
∎

In the same way one obtains the following

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
M\in C^{1,1}
and
\omega,d\omega,\delta\omega\in L^{p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
,
n\omega=0
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
.

Proof.

The proof repeats the previous case. We describe the differences. Now the identity (
7.36
) holds for all
\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
and
\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}(M,\Lambda)
. The condition (
7.39
) is replaced by

W_{I}=0,\quad I\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\quad\frac{\partial W_{I}}{\partial x^{n}}=0\quad\quad n\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}.

Recall that in this case
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
if
I
contains
n
. Then the test forms
\varphi=dW
,
\psi=\delta_{0}W
, defined in (
7.40
) satisfy
n\varphi=ndW=0
, and the rest goes through as above.
∎

Combining the Lemmas above, the proof of Theorem
7.15
is complete.

Remark 7.21
.

Similar to Lemmas
7.19
,
7.20
, one can also obtain corresponding statements for the Hodge-Dirac equation, thus providing an alternative “localized” proof of Theorems
6.27
,
6.28
. To this end, we first localize (
5.12
) which yields the relation

(\Omega,\alpha d\zeta+\delta\zeta)=(\xi f+d\xi\wedge\omega-\alpha d\xi\lrcorner\omega,\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
, compare (
7.37
) (we work here with the case of tangential boundary values). Now in local admissible coordinates, set
\zeta=dW+\alpha^{-1}\delta_{0}W
where
W\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}})
and
tW=0
on
x^{n}=0
. This gives

(\Omega,\delta dW+d\delta_{0}W+\alpha^{-1}\delta\delta_{0}W)=(\xi f+d\xi\wedge\omega-\alpha d\xi\lrcorner\omega,dW+\alpha^{-1}\delta W).

This integral identity is of the same form as the one used in the proof of Lemma
7.19
. The difference is that generally we can not split it into independent equations corresponding to homogeneous forms. Nevertheless, in local coordinates we have

\delta dW+d\delta_{0}W+\alpha^{-1}\delta\delta_{0}W+\triangle_{\mathrm{eucl}}W=(\delta-\delta_{0})dW+\alpha^{-1}(\delta-\delta_{0})\delta_{0}W

with the coefficients by the second derivatives in the RHS being small, so the rest of the proof of Lemma
7.19
goes modulo some heavier notation — we also have to sum integral identities over all
2(n+1)
homogeneous components.

7.7.
Refined local a priori estimates

In this Section we obtain refined local a priori estimates which may be of use of certain applications to low-regularity cases. We slightly change the solution representation obtained in Section
7.3
, keeping the same notation as above. This allows to obtain a refinement of the estimate obtained in Corollary
7.7
. First we show how to obtain the required result by using the estimate of Corollary
7.7
and duality. However, this estimate is not a local a priori estimate since it requires solving a dual problem.

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
M
be of the class
C^{1,1}
. Let
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
,
*\in\{0,T,N\}
satisfy (
4.5
) where
\varphi,\psi,\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
for any
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

(7.44)

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

Proof.

For
p_{-}\geq 2
this statement is already contained in Corollary
7.9
. Assume without loss that
p_{-}\leq 2
. We treat the Dirichlet problem, the other two cases being similar. Let
\xi,\mu\in L^{p_{-}^{\prime}}(M,\Lambda)
. By minimizing the functionals

\mathcal{F}_{1}[\Phi]=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}(\Phi,\Phi)-(\xi,d\Phi),\quad\mathcal{F}_{2}[\psi]=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}(\Psi,\Psi)-(\mu,d\Psi)

over
W^{1,2}_{T}(M,\Lambda)\cap(\mathcal{H}_{T}(M))^{\perp},
we find the minimizers satisfying

(7.45)

\mathcal{D}(\Phi,\zeta)=(\xi,d\Phi),\quad\mathcal{D}(\Psi,\zeta)=(\mu,d\Psi)

for all
\zeta\in W_{T}^{1,2}(M,\Lambda)
. In particular, we have

\mathcal{D}(\Phi,\Phi)\leq(\xi,\xi),\quad\mathcal{D}(\Psi,\Psi)\leq(\mu,\mu).

By (
5.5
),

(\Phi,\Phi)\leq C_{T}(\xi,\xi),\quad(\Psi,\Psi)\leq C_{T}(\mu,\mu).

By Corollary
7.9
we obtain
\Phi,\Psi\in W^{1,p_{-}^{\prime}}(M,\Lambda)
and

(7.46)

\|\Phi\|_{1,p_{-}^{\prime},M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\xi\|_{p_{-}^{\prime},M},\quad\|\Psi\|_{1,p_{-}^{\prime},M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\mu\|_{p_{-}^{\prime},M}).

Using approximation, the space of test forms
\zeta
in (
7.45
) can be extended to
W^{1,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. Thus

\displaystyle(\xi,d\omega)=\mathcal{D}(\Phi,\omega)=(\eta,\Phi)+(\varphi,d\Phi)+(\psi,\delta\Phi),

\displaystyle(\mu,\delta\omega)=\mathcal{D}(\Psi,\omega)=(\eta,\Psi)+(\varphi,d\Psi)+(\psi,\delta\Psi).

Using (
7.46
) we have

\displaystyle|(\xi,d\omega)|\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\xi\|_{p_{-}^{\prime},M}(\|\eta\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\varphi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\psi\|_{p_{-},M}),

\displaystyle|(\mu,\delta\omega)|\leq C(\mathrm{data})\|\mu\|_{p_{-}^{\prime},M}(\|\eta\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\varphi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\psi\|_{p_{-},M}).

By the duality characterization of the norm this yields

\displaystyle\|d\omega\|_{p_{-},M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\varphi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\psi\|_{p_{-},M}),

\displaystyle\|\delta\omega\|_{p_{-},M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\varphi\|_{p_{-},M}+\|\psi\|_{p_{-},M}).

Now we can use the Gaffney inequality of Lemma
7.16
and Corollary
7.7
to derive the inequality (
7.44
).
∎

In the following theorem we obtain the same result without using duality argument. This requires a different representation of terms arising at the localization stage in Section
7.3
. Namely, by using integraion-by-parts and special structure of the equation one can get rid of the derivatives of
\omega
on the right-hand side of the resulting equation.

Theorem 7.23
.

Let
M
be of the class
C^{1,1}
. Let
\omega\in W^{1,p_{-}}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
,
*\in\{0,T,N\}
satisfy (
4.5
) where
\varphi,\psi,\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
for any
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{*}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

(7.47)

\|\omega\|_{1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data})(\|\eta\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

If
M
is of the class
C^{s+1,1}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{s,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\eta\in W^{s-1.p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, then
\omega\in W^{s+1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and

(7.48)

\|\omega\|_{s+1,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\mathrm{data},s)(\|\eta\|_{s-1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{s,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{1,M}).

Proof.

We again assume that
\omega
is a homogeneous form.

Assuming the function
\xi
to be radial we get that
d\xi
is tangential at
x^{n}=0
and thus using the integration-by-parts formula one can rewrite

\displaystyle(-d\xi\lrcorner d\omega+d\xi\wedge\delta\omega,\zeta)

\displaystyle=-(d\omega,d\xi\wedge\zeta)+(\delta\omega,d\xi\lrcorner\zeta)

\displaystyle=(\omega,-\delta(d\xi\wedge\zeta)+d(d\xi\lrcorner\zeta)).

Note that the last expression does not contain derivatives of
\omega
, and with respect to
\zeta
it contains only the components of
\zeta
and their first derivatives. We can further transform this (though this is not essential) by writing

\displaystyle-\delta(d\xi\wedge\zeta)+d(d\xi\lrcorner\zeta)

\displaystyle=-\delta(d(\xi\zeta)-\xi d\zeta)-d(\delta(\xi\zeta)-\xi\delta\zeta)

\displaystyle=-\triangle(\xi\zeta)+\xi\triangle\zeta-d\xi\lrcorner d\zeta+d\xi\wedge\delta\zeta

and using that the Bochner-Weizenböck formula gives (see for instance
[
dR84
, Chapter V, §26]
)

-\triangle(\xi\zeta)+\xi\triangle\zeta=-\zeta\triangle\xi+2\nabla\xi\cdot\nabla\zeta,\quad(\nabla\xi\cdot\nabla\zeta)_{I}=(\nabla^{j}\xi)\nabla_{j}\zeta_{I},

the last expression containing only partial derivatives of
\zeta
of orders
0
and
1
, and the coefficients are expressed in terms of the metric tensor and Christoffel symbols only. Formally this calculation requires that the metric tensor be twice differentiable, but since its second order derivatives are not present in the final expression the relation readily extends to
C^{1,1}
Riemannian manifolds. Thus, instead of (
7.9
) after cancellations we get

(7.49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\omega\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=(\xi\varphi,d\zeta)+(\xi\psi,\delta\zeta)+(\hat{\eta},\zeta)-(\omega,\zeta)\triangle\xi+(\omega,2\nabla\xi\cdot\nabla\zeta),

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}

\displaystyle=\xi\eta+d\xi\lrcorner\varphi-d\xi\wedge\psi.

This means that instead of (
7.11
) we get a similar relation

(7.50)

\int\limits_{G_{2R}}\left[\nabla\Omega:\nabla\zeta+(\xi_{*}[(a(\cdot)-a(0))\nabla\Omega+b\Omega]+\widetilde{E})\nabla\zeta\right.\\
+\left.(\xi_{*}[b^{*}\nabla\Omega+c\Omega]+\widetilde{F})\zeta\right]\,dx=0,

for all
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{0},G_{2R})
, with
F
,
E
replaced by

\widetilde{F}=\xi f+e\nabla\xi+b_{1}\omega\nabla^{2}\xi+b_{2}\omega\nabla\xi,\quad\widetilde{E}=\xi e+a_{1}\omega\nabla\xi,

where

\displaystyle(a_{1}\omega\nabla\xi)\nabla\zeta=a_{1}^{IJ\alpha\beta}\omega_{J}\xi_{,\beta}\zeta_{I,\alpha},\quad a_{1}^{IJ\alpha\beta}=-2G^{IJ}g^{\alpha\beta}\sqrt{g},

\displaystyle(b_{1}\omega\nabla^{2}\xi)\zeta=b_{1}^{IJ\alpha\beta}\omega_{J}\xi_{,\alpha\beta}\zeta_{I},\quad b_{1}^{IJ\alpha\beta}=-g^{\alpha\beta}G^{IJ}\sqrt{g}=\frac{1}{2}a_{1}^{IJ\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle(b_{2}\omega\nabla\xi)\zeta=b_{2}^{IJ\alpha}\omega_{J}\xi_{,\alpha}\zeta_{I}.

Here
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
depend only on the coefficients of the metric, and thus on
C^{s+1,1}
manifold belong to the class
C^{s,1}(\widetilde{G}_{2R})
, while
b_{2}
also depends on the derivatives of the metric tensor, and thus belong to
C^{s-1,1}(G_{2R})
if
s>0
and
L^{\infty}(G_{2R})
if
s=0
. The corresponding bounds are uniform with respect to
R
and to the element of the corresponding cover (
R
-atlas).

Arguing as above, we obtain the integral equation (for
n=2
this requires the same modification as in (
7.18
))

\Omega-T[\Omega]=\mathcal{P}[\widetilde{F}]+\mathcal{Q}[\widetilde{E}]

and instead of (
7.20
) we get

\Omega=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}T^{j}(\mathcal{P}[\widetilde{F}]+\mathcal{Q}[\widetilde{E}]).

Therefore, choosing sufficiently small
R=R(\mathrm{data},s)
as above (see Lemmas
7.4
,
7.11
) so that
T
is a contraction in the norm
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}
with the norm
\leq 1/2
for all
k=1,\ldots,s+1
, we get

\|\omega\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{R}}\leq 2\|\mathcal{P}[\widetilde{F}]\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+2\|\mathcal{Q}[\widetilde{E}]\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

We start from
s=0
. In this case all the coefficients of
a_{1}
,
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
are uniformly bounded and using Lemma
7.1
we evaluate

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[\widetilde{F}]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq CR\|\widetilde{F}\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq CR\|f\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+C\|e\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+CR^{-1}\|\omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}[\widetilde{E}]\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\widetilde{E}\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}\leq C\|e\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+CR^{-1}\|\omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}.

Thus

\|\omega\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{R}}\leq C(R\|f\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|e\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+R^{-1}\|\omega\|_{p(\cdot),G_{2R}}).

Gluing these estimates together and using interpolation we obtain (
7.47
).

Now let
s>0
. If
k>1
, using again Lemmas
7.2
,
7.10
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}[\widetilde{F}]\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|^{*}_{k-2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+C\|e\|^{*}_{k-1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+CR^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{k-1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}[\widetilde{E}]\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\xi e+a_{1}\omega\nabla\xi\|^{*}_{k-1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|e\|^{*}_{k-1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+CR^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{k-1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}

Thus we have

\|\omega\|^{*}_{k,p(\cdot),G_{R}}\leq C(\|f\|^{*}_{k-2,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+\|e\|^{*}_{k-1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}+R^{-1}\|\omega\|^{*}_{k-1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}})

which implies

\|\omega\|_{k,p(\cdot),M}\leq C(\|\eta\|_{k-2,p(\cdot),M}+\|\varphi\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\psi\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}+\|\omega\|_{k-1,p(\cdot),M}).

Iterating this estimate in
k=1,\ldots,s+1
and using interpolation we get (
7.48
).
∎

7.8.
Additional regularity of the potential on
C^{1,1}
manifolds

In this Section we prove that on
C^{1,1}
manifold for
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
the Dirichlet/Neumann potentials
G_{D}[\eta]
,
G_{N}[\eta]
, which belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, have differential and codifferential also from
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
.

Theorem 7.24
.

Let
\omega_{0},d\omega_{0}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\omega\in\omega_{0}+W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
), that is

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)=(\eta,\zeta)+(\varphi,d\zeta)+(\psi,\delta\zeta),

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy
t\alpha=t\psi
,
t\beta=td\omega_{0}
,

(7.51)

d\alpha+\delta\beta=\eta+d\psi+\delta\varphi

and

(7.52)

\mathcal{D}(\alpha,\zeta)=(\eta+d\psi,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(\beta,\zeta)=(\eta+\delta\varphi,\delta\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
.

Proof.

Let
\omega
be a homogeneous form of degree
r
and
\Omega=\omega\xi
. Let the cut-off functions
\xi
,
\xi_{*}
be defined as in Sections
7.3
–
7.5
There holds (see(
7.9
))

\mathcal{D}(\Omega,\zeta)=(\widetilde{\eta},\zeta)+(\widetilde{\varphi},d\zeta)+(\widetilde{\psi},\delta\zeta)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}=\xi\varphi+d\xi\wedge\omega,\quad\widetilde{\psi}=\xi\psi-d\xi\lrcorner\omega,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}=\xi\eta+d\xi\wedge(\delta\omega-\psi)-d\xi\lrcorner(d\omega-\varphi)

Here
\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{\psi}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\widetilde{\eta}\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
.
This could be further transformed to a somewhat simpler expression (see (
7.49
)) but we do not need this form here.

Further we shall work with boundary coordinate charts, the interior case being much simpler. Recall that for tangential boundary values
I\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
iff
n\notin I
. Take
\zeta=dW
where

(7.53)

W\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}),\quad W_{I}(x^{\prime},0)=0\quad\text{if}\quad n\notin I,\quad\frac{\partial W_{I}(x^{\prime},0)}{\partial x^{n}}=0\quad\text{if}\quad n\in I.

We obtain (note that here we work in one particular coordinate chart)

(7.54)

(\delta\Omega,\delta dW)=(\widetilde{\psi},\delta dW)+(\widetilde{\eta},dW)

Now, the form which is defined as
\delta_{0}W
in our local coordinate system, and denoted as such:

(\delta_{0}W)_{I}=-W_{jI,j},

satisfies
t\delta_{0}W=0
and thus the integration-by-parts formula yields

(7.55)

(\delta\Omega,d\delta_{0}W)=0.

Summing (
7.54
) and (
7.55
) we arive at

(7.56)

(\delta\Omega,\delta dW+d\delta_{0}W)=(\widetilde{\psi},\delta dW)+(\widetilde{\eta},dW).

Denote
\delta\Omega=\Psi
. In coordinates (
7.56
) becomes

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}G^{IJ}_{r}

\displaystyle\Psi_{I}[(\delta d+d\delta_{0})W]_{J}\sqrt{g}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}G^{IJ}_{r-1}\widetilde{\psi}_{I}(\delta dW)_{J}\sqrt{g}\,dx+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}G^{IJ}_{r}\widetilde{\eta}_{I}(dW)_{J}\sqrt{g}\,dx.

Rewrite this as

-\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\Psi_{I}\triangle W_{I}\,dx=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\xi_{*}G^{IJ}_{r}\Psi_{I}[(\delta_{0}-\delta)dW]_{J}\sqrt{g}\,dx\\
+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\xi_{*}(G^{IJ}_{r}\sqrt{g}-G^{IJ}(0))\Psi_{I}\triangle W_{J}\,dx\\
+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}G^{IJ}_{r-1}\widetilde{\psi}_{I}(\delta dW)_{J}\sqrt{g}\,dx+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}G^{IJ}_{r}\widetilde{\eta}_{I}(dW)_{J}\sqrt{g}\,dx.

We further rewrite this as

(7.57)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\Psi_{I}\triangle W_{I}\,dx=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}F^{I\beta}

\displaystyle W_{I,\beta}dx+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}C^{I\alpha\beta}W_{I,\alpha\beta}dx

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\xi_{*}\Psi_{J}(A^{IJ\alpha\beta}W_{I,\alpha\beta}+B^{IJ\alpha}W_{I,\alpha})\,dx,

where
A^{IJ\alpha\beta}
,
B^{IJ\alpha}
satisfy (
7.41
), the coefficients
C^{I\alpha\beta}
,
F^{I\beta}
vanish outside
G_{2R}
, and
C^{I\alpha\beta}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
,
F^{I\beta}\in L^{p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
.

Then by Lemma
7.18
and our choice (
7.53
) of
W_{I}
we get

\displaystyle\Psi_{I}

\displaystyle=Q_{\alpha}^{D}[F^{I\alpha}+B^{IJ\alpha}\xi_{*}\Psi_{J}]+H_{\alpha\beta}^{D}[\xi_{*}A^{IJ\alpha\beta}\Omega_{J}+C^{I\alpha\beta}],\quad I\in\mathcal{I}_{0},

\displaystyle\Psi_{I}

\displaystyle=Q_{\alpha}^{N}[F^{I\alpha}+B^{IJ\alpha}\xi_{*}\Omega_{J}]+H_{\alpha\beta}^{N}[\xi_{*}A^{IJ\alpha\beta}\Psi_{J}+C^{I\alpha\beta}],\quad I\notin\mathcal{I}_{0}.

We abbreviate this to

\Psi=\mathcal{Q}[F]+\mathcal{H}[C]+\mathcal{Q}[\xi_{*}B\Psi]+\mathcal{H}[\xi_{*}A\Psi].

Note that by Lemmas
3.14
,
3.15
there holds
\mathcal{Q}[F],\mathcal{H}(C)\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
. Using the contraction property of the operator
\mathcal{T}_{0}
(see (
7.43
) with
p_{-}
replaced by
p(\cdot)
) we get

\Psi=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{0})^{j}(Q[F]+\mathcal{H}(C))

first in
L^{p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
and then in
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(G_{2R})
(with the norm
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{1,p(\cdot),G_{2R}}
). Thus we get
\delta\Omega=\Psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
, which immediately implies
\delta G_{D}[\eta]=\delta\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
.

To prove that
d\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
we take first
\zeta=\delta_{0}W
, which gives

(7.58)

(d\Omega,d\delta_{0}W)=-(\delta\Omega,\delta\delta_{0}W)+(\widetilde{\varphi},d\delta_{0}W)+(\widetilde{\psi},\delta\delta_{0}W)+(\widetilde{\eta},\delta_{0}W).

On the other hand, by the integration-by-parts formula,

(7.59)

(d\Omega,\delta_{0}dW)=(d\Omega,(\delta_{0}-\delta)dW)+(d\Omega_{0},\delta dW),\quad\Omega_{0}=\xi\omega_{0}.

Finally, combining (
7.58
), (
7.59
), and denoting
d\Omega=\Phi
, we get

(7.60)

\displaystyle(\Phi,(d\delta_{0}W+\delta_{0}dW))

\displaystyle=(\Psi,(\delta_{0}-\delta)\delta_{0}W)+(\Phi,(\delta_{0}-\delta)dW)+(\widetilde{\varphi},d\delta_{0}W)

\displaystyle+(\widetilde{\psi},(\delta-\delta_{0})\delta_{0}W)+(\widetilde{\eta},\delta_{0}W)+(d\Omega_{0},\delta dW).

In coordinates (
7.60
) takes the form similar to (
7.57
):

(7.61)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\Phi_{I}\triangle W_{I}\,dx=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\widetilde{F}^{I\beta}

\displaystyle W_{I,\beta}dx+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\widetilde{C}^{I\alpha\beta}W_{I,\alpha\beta}dx

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\xi_{*}\Phi_{J}(A^{IJ\alpha\beta}W_{I,\alpha\beta}+B^{IJ\alpha}W_{I,\alpha})\,dx,

where the coefficients
\widetilde{F}^{I\beta}
,
\widetilde{C}^{I\alpha\beta}
have the same properties as
F^{I\beta}
,
C^{I\alpha\beta}
. The rest follows as above, and we get
d\Omega=\Phi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
.

Therefore,
d\omega,\delta\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. Using the integration-by-parts formula
we get

(\delta(d\omega)+d(\delta\omega)-\eta-d\psi-\delta\varphi,\zeta)=[\delta\omega-\psi,\zeta]

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
and thus

(7.62)

\delta(d\omega)+d(\delta\omega)=\eta+d\psi+\delta\varphi,\quad t(\delta\omega-\psi)=0.

Now, by Lemma
2.8
and (
7.62
) we have

(d(\delta\omega),d\zeta)=(\eta+d\psi+\delta\varphi-\delta(d\omega),d\zeta)=(\eta+d\psi,d\zeta)

and

(\delta(d\omega),\delta\zeta)=(\eta+d\psi+\delta\varphi-d(\delta\omega),\delta\eta)=(\eta+d\psi+\delta\varphi-d\psi,\delta\zeta)=(\eta+\delta\varphi,\delta\zeta).

Since
d(d\omega)=0
,
\delta(\delta\omega)=0
, this proves (
7.52
).
∎

We state the following corollary for Dirichlet potentials.

Lemma 7.25
.

If
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
with
\mathcal{P}_{T}[\eta]=0
, then for
\alpha=\delta G_{D}[\eta]
and
\beta=dG_{D}[\eta]
we have
\alpha,\beta\in W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and they satisfy

(7.63)

d\alpha+\delta\beta=\eta

and

(7.64)

\mathcal{D}(\alpha,\zeta)=(\eta,d\zeta),\quad\mathcal{D}(\beta,\zeta)=(\eta,\delta\zeta)

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
. As a corollary, if additionally
\eta\in W^{\delta,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
then
\delta G_{D}[\eta]=G_{D}[\delta\eta]
and if additionally
\eta\in W^{d,p_{-}}_{T}(M,\Lambda)
then
dG_{D}[\eta]=G_{D}[d\eta]
.

Proof.

From Theorem
7.24
it follows that
\alpha,\beta\in W_{T}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy (
7.63
),(
7.64
). It remains to see that
\mathcal{P}_{T}\alpha,\mathcal{P}_{T}\beta=0
, the first relation in (
7.64
) coincides with the relation defining the Dirichlet potential of
\delta\eta
and the second relation in (
7.64
) coincides with the relation defining the Dirichlet potential of
d\eta
if
t\eta=0
.
∎

The following result and its corollary for Neumann potentials is obtained in exactly the same manner or by the Hodge duality

Theorem 7.26
.

Let
\omega_{0},\delta\omega_{0}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\omega\in\omega_{0}+W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
), that is

\mathcal{D}(\omega,\zeta)=(\eta,\zeta)+(\varphi,d\zeta)+(\psi,\delta\zeta),

for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy
n\alpha=n\delta\omega_{0}
,
n\beta=n\varphi
, (
7.51
), and (
7.52
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
.

In this case
I\in\mathcal{I_{0}}
iff
n\in I
and we take
W\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}})
such that

(7.65)

W_{I}(x^{\prime},0)=0\quad\text{if}\quad n\in I,\quad\frac{\partial W_{I}}{\partial x^{n}}(x^{\prime},0)\quad\text{if}\quad n\notin I.

Then
ndW=0
,
n\delta_{0}W=0
. The relation (
7.54
) takes exactly the same form, the relation (
7.55
) becomes

(\delta\Omega,d\delta_{0}W)=(\delta\Omega_{0},d\delta_{0}W),\quad\Omega_{0}=\xi\omega_{0},

and thus instead of (
7.56
) we arrive at (denoting
\Psi=\delta\Omega
)

(7.66)

(\Psi,\delta dW+d\delta_{0}W)=(\widetilde{\psi},\delta dW)+(\widetilde{\eta},dW)+(\delta\omega_{0},d\delta_{0}W).

This yields
\Psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(\Omega,\Lambda)
. Now, for
\Phi=d\Omega
instead of (
7.59
) we have

(d\Omega,\delta_{0}dW)=(d\Omega,(\delta_{0}-\delta)dW)

and (
7.60
) is replaced by

(\Phi,(d\delta_{0}+\delta_{0}d)W)=-(\Psi,(\delta-\delta_{0})\delta_{0}W)+(\widetilde{\varphi},d\delta_{0}W)\\
+(\widetilde{\psi},(\delta-\delta_{0})\delta_{0}W)+(\widetilde{\eta},\delta_{0}W).

This relation yields
\Phi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(\Omega,\Lambda)
.

Lemma 7.27
.

If
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
with
\mathcal{P}_{N}[\eta]=0
, then for
\alpha=\delta G_{N}[\eta]
and
\beta=dG_{N}[\eta]
we have
\alpha,\beta\in W_{N}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and they satisfy (
7.63
) and (
7.64
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
. As a corollary, if additionally
\eta\in W^{d,p_{-}}(M,\Lambda)
then
dG_{N}[\eta]=G_{N}[d\eta]
and if additionally
\eta\in W^{\delta,p_{-}}_{N}(M,\Lambda)
then
\delta G_{N}[\eta]=G_{N}[\delta\eta]
.

For the problem with natural boundary conditions we have the following result:

Theorem 7.28
.

Let
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
both belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy
t\alpha=t\psi
,
n\beta=n\varphi
, (
7.51
), and (
7.52
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}(M,\Lambda)
.

In this case to establish the regularity of
\delta\omega
we repeat the first part of the proof of Theorem
7.24
and use the set of test forms described in (
7.53
), which gives (
7.56
), and finally leads to
\delta\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. To establish the regularity if
d\omega
we use the set of test forms (
7.65
) corresponding to the proof of Theorem
7.26
which leads to (
7.60
) and by the same arguments this yields
d\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
. The rest follows from integration-by-parts.

While formally the set of test forms in relations (
7.52
) for
\alpha
and
\beta
in Theorem
7.28
is free of any boundary values, in fact the relation for
\alpha
essentially requires only test forms with vanishing tangential part and in the relation for
\beta
one can use only test forms with vanishing normal part. Indeed, if the first of the relations in (
7.52
) holds for test forms with vanishing tangential component then replacing
\zeta
by
\zeta-d\gamma
with
nd\gamma=n\zeta
,
t\gamma=0
and
n\gamma=0
(see Corollary
3.18
) we immediately see that it holds for all sufficiently regular test forms without any assumption on the boundary values. The same for the second relation in (
7.52
), here one can take
\zeta-\delta\gamma
instead of
\zeta
, with
t\delta\gamma=t\zeta
,
t\gamma=0
,
n\gamma=0
.

In case of the full Dirichlet data the potentials
\alpha,\beta\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy (
7.51
). But for the boundary regularity we do not have results similar to the above. To see what the complication here is, assume that
\omega_{0},d\omega_{0},\delta\omega_{0}\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\omega\in\omega_{0}+W_{0}^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
. If these potentials had the required regularity
\alpha,\beta\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
(this is definitely so if
M
is
C^{2,1}
), we would clearly have
n\alpha=n\delta\omega_{0}
,
t\beta=td\omega_{0}
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\alpha,\zeta)=(\eta+d\psi+\delta\varphi-\delta\beta,d\zeta)\quad\text{for all}\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}(M,\Lambda),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\beta,\zeta)=(\eta+d\psi+\delta\varphi-d\alpha,\delta\zeta)\quad\text{for all}\quad\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}(M,\Lambda).

In particular, we would have

\displaystyle\alpha=\delta\omega_{0}+\delta G_{N}[\eta-\mathcal{P}_{N}\eta+d(\psi-\delta\omega_{0})+\delta(\varphi-\beta)],

\displaystyle\beta=d\omega_{0}+dG_{D}[\eta-\mathcal{P}_{T}\eta+d(\psi-\alpha)+\delta(\varphi-d\omega_{0})].

Unlike the relations for the Dirichlet and Neumann potentials, here the right-hand side contains
d\alpha
and
\delta\beta
. Thus in this case we state only an interior regularity result.

Theorem 7.29
.

Let
\omega\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
,
\eta\in L^{p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
and
\varphi,\psi\in W^{1,p(\cdot)}(M,\Lambda)
satisfy (
4.5
) for all
\zeta\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}(M,\Lambda)
. Then
\alpha=\delta\omega
and
\beta=d\omega
belong to
W^{1,p(\cdot)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M,\Lambda)
and satisfy
d\alpha+\delta\beta=\beta+d\psi+\delta\varphi
a.e. in
M
.
